% remain reachable through the PDF bookmarks, and listing them made the
% contents run to three pages.
\setcounter{tocdepth}{2}
\tableofcontents
\clearpage

%% --- begin sections_v07/01-introduction ---
\section{Introduction}
\label{sec:intro}

Six-player Canadian Fish, also called Literature, is an imperfect-information game played by two
teams of three. Players observe their own cards and the public action history, but not other
players' hands. Because every card transfer is public, a candidate initial deal and the observed
history determine subsequent card ownership. This structure permits exact enumeration of deals
satisfying observer-specific game constraints, providing a reference calculation for
constraint-based inference.

Strategic action choices can convey information beyond legality constraints. We therefore
distinguish the uniform constraint-conditioned distribution from a policy-aware posterior.
\sestina{} uses an approximate belief model with behavioural weighting and combines it with a linear
action policy and guarded search. The study evaluates this configuration against specified internal
baselines, examines component contributions, and assesses sensitivity to partners, rule variants,
and tested adversarial responses.

Computational work on the six-player game appears to be limited. Within the search scope stated
in \S\ref{sec:related-fish} we did not identify academic work on it, a public repository containing
search, planning or a working learner for it, or coverage in RLCard \cite{rlcard}. The nearest
agent we identified plays a four-player, 48-card variant that does not raise the six-player
allocation problem.

This paper presents \textbf{\sestina{}}, a configuration developed from FishBot \vsix{}, and
evaluates it using the protocol described in \S\ref{sec:evaluation}. Earlier versions established
the engine, the constraint-based inference machinery and a sequence of progressively stronger
policies, but their development also exposed evaluation problems: comparisons that could not resolve
a one-point effect, an optimiser that could not move a policy, and at least two published
conclusions that turned out to be artefacts of the measurement rather than properties of the agent
(\S\ref{sec:background}). The question this paper addresses is whether the tested modifications
improve performance under the revised evaluation protocol.

\paragraph{Version nomenclature.} \sestina{} was developed and evaluated under the identifier
FishBot v0.7. The released agent retains the frozen configuration; source paths, experiment
identifiers, seeds, deal banks, digests and configuration strings retain their original
\texttt{v07} names for reproducibility, including the frozen configuration
\texttt{v07:r12=25,\ldots} of \S\ref{sec:agent} and the sealed banks of \S\ref{sec:evaluation}.
Throughout, \emph{\sestina{}} names the agent and \emph{the v0.7 cycle} names the programme that
produced and measured it. Appendix~\ref{app:repro} gives the detailed mapping.

\subsection{Research questions}
\label{sec:intro-rq}

\begin{description}
\item[RQ1] Does \sestina{} achieve a replicated improvement over \fcheap{}, the registered
  baseline (\S\ref{sec:development-frontier}), measured on material sealed before \sestina{}
  existed?
\item[RQ2] Does that improvement survive changes of partner, cross-play against separately trained
  policies, alternative rule variants, and adversarial responses fitted against it?
\item[RQ3] Which of \sestina{}'s changes account for the improvement, and which candidate
  mechanisms fail to produce measurable strength?
\item[RQ4] What do the results imply about the headroom remaining in the current policy class?
\end{description}

\S\ref{sec:results} answers RQ1 and RQ2 directly, \S\ref{sec:development} and
\S\ref{sec:results-attribution} answer RQ3, and \S\ref{sec:discussion} takes up RQ4.

\subsection{Scope of comparative claims}
\label{sec:intro-strength}

This paper makes comparative claims of three different kinds, and they are kept separate throughout.

\begin{description}
\item[Within-project comparisons.] \sestina{} is compared with its own predecessors and with the
  \emph{\vsix{} frontier}, by which this paper means the set of \vsix{}-era configurations available
  at the end of that cycle: the deployed non-searching policy and the searching configurations
  \fcheap{} and \fmid{}, ordered by search cost (\S\ref{sec:development-frontier}). The paper
  establishes an improvement over the registered baseline \fcheap{}. It does not establish that
  \sestina{} is the strongest configuration in the lineage on every metric: its comparison with a
  development-time composite is unresolved, and on empirical minimax regret over a shared panel it
  ranks third of four (\S\ref{sec:results-panel}).
\item[Robustness evaluation.] Whether the improvement persists against a fixed opponent panel, under
  substituted partners, across rule variants, and against adversarial responses fitted to the
  agent. The paper evaluates these and reports mixed results.
\item[External comparisons.] Whether \sestina{} is the strongest Canadian Fish agent anyone has
  built. The paper does not establish this and does not claim it. The comparison class contains only
  agents this project wrote, and every adversarial-response figure here is the observed edge of a
  particular search rather than a bound on what a search could find.
\end{description}

Accordingly the paper does not describe \sestina{} as near-optimal, unexploitable, or the
strongest known Fish-playing team. \S\ref{sec:discussion-future} states what evidence those claims
would require.

\subsection{Contributions}
\label{sec:intro-contributions}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Constraint-based inference} (\S\ref{sec:inference}): a half-suit factorisation and
  count dynamic program for the normalisation constant, card-location marginals, and
  declaration-allocation probabilities under the uniform feasible-deal model.

\item \textbf{Agent specification} (\S\ref{sec:agent}): a reproducible configuration combining
  approximate inference with behavioural weighting, linear action scoring, public-history
  tie-breaking, half-suit contestation, stall detection, and guarded search.

\item \textbf{Evaluation design} (\S\ref{sec:evaluation}): duplicate-deal comparisons, held-out
  deal banks, preregistered criteria, uncertainty estimation, adversarial-search calibration, and
  information-access tests.

\item \textbf{Empirical findings} (\S\ref{sec:results}, \S\ref{sec:development-rejected}): a
  positive comparison with the registered baseline, an unresolved comparison with the development
  composite, component ablations, sensitivity analyses with explicitly stated limitations, and four
  candidate modifications that did not demonstrate an additional improvement under the tested
  conditions.
\end{enumerate}
%% --- end sections_v07/01-introduction ---
%% --- begin sections_v07/02-game ---
\section{Six-player Canadian Fish}
\label{sec:game}

This section specifies the evaluated game variant and then describes the computational and
informational structure that motivates the study. Readers who know the game should still read
\S\ref{sec:game-dialect}, because published descriptions differ on several consequential points and
the rule set studied here is one explicit selection among them. Appendix~\ref{app:dialect} lists
the rule choices against the engine switches that change them.

\subsection{Rules}
\label{sec:game-rules}

Six players form two teams of three, seated alternately so that no two teammates are adjacent. The
deck is 54 cards: the standard 52 plus two jokers. It is partitioned into nine \textbf{half-suits} of
six cards each, being for each suit the low half $2$ through $7$ and the high half $9$ through $A$,
plus a ninth half-suit made of the four eights and the two jokers. Every card belongs to exactly one
half-suit, and each player is dealt nine. The team that takes at least five of the nine half-suits
wins.

Play proceeds by \textbf{asks}. On their turn a player names one opponent and one card. The ask is
legal only if the named card lies in a half-suit that has not yet been claimed, the asker holds at
least one \emph{other} card of that half-suit, the asker does \emph{not} hold the named card, and the
target is an opponent who still holds at least one card. If the target holds the card they must
surrender it and the asker keeps the turn; otherwise the turn passes to the target. The first turn
belongs to the seat after a randomly chosen dealer. When the turn reaches a seat that holds no
cards, that seat names a teammate who still holds cards to receive it; the engine substitutes the
lowest-index such teammate if the named one is ineligible, and if no teammate holds cards the
opposing team enters the forced endgame described in \S\ref{sec:game-dialect}. A seat whose turn it
is and that has no legal ask must declare, and does so on the lowest-index unclaimed half-suit in
which it holds a card. The requirement
that an asker already hold a card of the half-suit makes each legal ask informative about the
asker's holdings, because it turns every ask into a public declaration of partial ownership.

A half-suit is claimed by a \textbf{declaration}. A declaring player names, for each of the six cards
of a half-suit, which member of their \emph{own} team holds it. The claim is all-or-nothing: if every
assignment is correct the declaring team scores the half-suit, and if any assignment is wrong the
opposing team scores it. A declaration is legal only while the half-suit is unclaimed. In the evaluated variant any seat
may declare, in or out of turn and whether or not it holds cards, at any point between two public
events; when several seats propose at the same point, the lowest seat index across both teams
declares first and the others are polled again once that declaration has resolved. A declaration
does not change whose turn it is.

Actions, outcomes, transfers, declarations and hand sizes are public; the identities of the cards
in a player's hand remain private unless a transfer or declaration reveals them. Every ask, its
answer, every card transfer, every declaration and its outcome, and every player's card count are
observed by all six players, so the only hidden information is the initial deal, and the public
record determines every later card position given that deal.

\subsection{Evaluated rule variant}
\label{sec:game-dialect}

Published descriptions of this game document mutually incompatible variants \cite{pagat}, so the rule
set below is one specified variant among the published rule sets. It matches the strategy corpus
this line of work draws on \cite{develin} and the rule set adopted for this implementation. Six of
the eight contested choices Appendix~\ref{app:dialect} lists are engine switches, so their effect
can be measured, and \S\ref{sec:results-dialects} measures the principal comparison over
\vsevenDiaRowsWord{} rule-variant rows: the default and seven variations. The misdeclaration award
is hardcoded and was not varied; the forced-endgame ladder has no flag of its own inside the scored
harness and moves only inside the \texttt{-{}-legacy} bundle, which is why
\S\ref{sec:results-dialects} can estimate it only as a residual. The consequential choices are a
54-card deck in nine half-suits with nine cards per player; declaration legal at any moment, in or out
of turn, by a seat holding cards or not; a misdeclaration awarding the half-suit to the opposing team;
lowest seat index first among simultaneous declarers; an eight-level willingness ladder in the
\textbf{forced endgame} that arises when one whole team is cardless; and a 400-ask action cap whose
residue is adjudicated by physical majority. In the forced endgame the team that still holds cards
must declare every remaining half-suit: the engine polls that team's seats down a ladder of
correctness-probability thresholds, $0.995$, $0.98$, $0.95$, $0.90$, $0.80$, $0.65$ and $0.50$,
followed by a compulsory level at which the first polled seat declares its best guess; at each
level every unclaimed half-suit and every seat of the team is polled in index order, the first
willing seat declares, and the sweep restarts from the top level for the next half-suit. At the
action cap each unclaimed half-suit is awarded to the team physically holding more of its six
cards, and a 3--3 split is awarded to the team holding the lowest-index card of the half-suit; every
half-suit is awarded whole, so a nine-half-suit game cannot tie. In the 48-card, eight-half-suit
variant of \S\ref{sec:results-dialects} each player is dealt eight cards and a 4--4 game is recorded
by the harness as a win for the team seated at index~0; because every deal is played once in each
seating, this is symmetric in expectation across the two configurations, but a tie is not scored as
a half win. No prearranged signalling conventions are available to any policy, and team
communication is limited to the forced-endgame willingness bit and to the successor a cardless
turn-holder names. Appendix~\ref{app:dialect} lists each choice against the switch that changes it.

Two rules are places where the engine is deliberately \emph{more} restrictive than the adopted
rule set allows, and both close coordination channels a policy could otherwise exploit. We evaluated
the two restrictions using the interventions described below. When the turn reaches a cardless seat
whose teammates both hold cards, the rules permit open negotiation over who receives it; the engine
instead has the cardless seat choose unilaterally. A genuine multi-candidate transfer decision
arises \numTransferEvents{} times per game. Replacing the unilateral rule on one team with a
chooser that, given the true deal, hands the turn to the teammate with the most immediately certain
pulls, paired on common deals, changed the mean half-suit score by
$\numTransferDelta \pm \numTransferCI$ half-suits per game (mean and 95\% half-width) over
\numTransferGames{} paired \vfour{} mirror games, in which the two rules diverged in
\numTransferDiverged\% of games. The tested intervention therefore produced no resolved change in
mean half-suit score. This is an oracle-assisted diagnostic rather than a bound on every legal
mechanism: the clairvoyant chooser is a greedy heuristic that does not optimise game value, so the
interval prices the channel for that class of chooser, and it was measured on an earlier policy
rather than on \sestina{}. Similarly, the engine arbitrates simultaneous declarations by lowest seat
index rather than by stated confidence, because a confidence comparison would move a statistic of
one seat's hand to five others. That restriction costs $+\numArbCost{}$~pp of win rate pooled over
\numArbGames{} games on five seeds, and an arbitrator that, given the true deal, selects a correct
declaration from those proposed is worth \numArbOracle{}~pp pooled over three of those seeds. Both
figures measure the effects of two diagnostic arbitration interventions over the fixed proposal
set: the oracle selects a correct proposal when one exists and does not optimise continuation
value, so neither figure bounds the value attainable by arbitration over that set, nor the value of
a policy that changes what it proposes. A half-suit differential and a win-rate edge are different units and are
not converted into one another here.

\subsection{Computational and informational structure}
\label{sec:game-why}

\paragraph{Hidden-state representation.}
Because a card changes hands only through an observed ask, the only thing any player does not know is
the initial deal, and the public record determines every subsequent card position given that deal. In
many hidden-information games the object that must be filtered is the set of reachable
\emph{histories}, for which constructing even one consistent history is FNP-complete in the worst
case, with efficient enumeration available exactly when the public tree is sparse
\cite{solinas-history}. The representation used here reduces hidden-state reconstruction to
constraints on the initial deal: there is exactly one object to reconstruct, and the constraints the
rules impose on it are logical rather than probabilistic. A player told ``I do not hold the seven of
hearts'' knows it, permanently and with certainty. Throughout, a \emph{certificate} is such a hard
constraint on card location derived from a public event.

The research consequence is that Fish permits the constraint-based part of inference to be
\emph{separated} from the policy. Exact enumeration of the deals consistent with the certificates
(\S\ref{sec:inference}) provides a reference for the constraint-based inference computation. It
does not remove uncertainty about the behavioural model used to interpret strategic actions: a
strategically chosen ask can make some feasible deals more likely than others, and that weighting
is a modelling choice the reference calculation does not make (\S\ref{sec:inference-prior}). The
separation isolates, for this paper's negative results, the uncertainty that lies in the policy and
the behavioural model rather than in the certificate arithmetic. It also motivates the belief-model
comparison reported in \S\ref{sec:background-vsix}, in which the exact constraint-based model is a
worse predictor of card location than the deployed approximation with behavioural weighting.

\paragraph{Decentralised team decision-making.}
Three teammates share a payoff, hold different private information, and have no communication channel
beyond their public actions. This is the decentralised control problem the common-information
approach formalises, in which a notional coordinator who knows the common information issues
\emph{prescriptions} mapping each controller's private information to an action
\cite{nayyar2013common}. In Fish the common information is the entire public history, which
induces a common-information belief over the joint private hands. \sestina{} instead maintains an
observer-conditioned belief that combines a seat's private hand with the public history; this is
related to, but not identical with, the coordinator belief used in common-information formulations.
The coordinator's problem is not solvable here, since a prescription maps every possible nine-card hand to
an ask, so the reduction serves as a correctness lens rather than an algorithm. What it does yield
cheaply is a property the cooperative-search literature has to work for \cite{lerer2020sparta}: the
mapping implemented by a \emph{deterministic} procedure reading only public state and its own hand
is common knowledge among the three teammates, with no shared seed and no communication, although
the action it produces at another seat is not, since that seat's hand is private. The determinism
qualifier matters, and \S\ref{sec:agent-tiebreak} is where it becomes an engine switch.

\paragraph{Public policy specification.}
In the extensive-form team-game models this paper cites, a team coordinates through an ex-ante
correlation device: a joint strategy is sampled before play, the opponent may know the distribution
it is sampled from, and the realised sample is private to the team
\cite{celli-gatti,farina-collusion,zhang-tbdag}. A team of three identical, deterministic, published
agents differs from that model in two respects: the policy mapping is public, and there is no
private sampled signal. Knowledge of the policy does not reveal an action determined by a private
hand, but it lets an opponent compute the distribution of a seat's action from the public record
(\S\ref{sec:agent-coordination}). \S\ref{sec:threat} adopts that as the threat model, and
\S\ref{sec:results-adversarial} reports what an adversary that reads the target's policy and
inverts its transcript achieved. Within the search scope described in \S\ref{sec:related-fish}, we
did not identify prior work that addresses this case.

\paragraph{Computational cost.}
A deal resolves in roughly a hundred public events, and on one \vsevenMirrorThreads{}-thread
desktop the engine plays a six-seat mirror of the \emph{blueprint} policy, the non-searching base
policy that the test-time search of \S\ref{sec:agent-search} modifies, at
\vsevenMirrorGamesPerSec{} complete games per second, with the deployed belief running in every
seat; the evaluated configuration, which searches, shows an observed whole-match slowdown of
\vsevenCostFrozenTight$\times$ relative to the non-searching blueprint (\S\ref{sec:results-cost}). This is what makes
the evaluation programme of \S\ref{sec:evaluation} possible: \vsevenBtwoFcheapN{} paired games for a
single headline comparison, two disjoint holdout banks for every claim, a \vsevenPanelMembers{}-member
opponent panel scored identically for four configurations, and \vsevenBfourArmsWord{} separately
fitted adversarial searches all run on one desktop. In a domain where one evaluation cell costs
GPU-hours, the version of this study that reports its own negative results could not have been
afforded. The corresponding liability appears in \S\ref{sec:discussion-external}: low simulation
cost does not provide independent validation.

\paragraph{Planning limitations.}
Two limitations bear on how later results should be read. First, the perfect-information version of
this game offers little to a clairvoyant planner. A player who sees every hand names only cards the
target holds, so never fails an ask, never loses the turn, and declares each half-suit as soon as
their team holds all of it; under the implemented rules the team on turn then takes every half-suit
not dealt outright to the opponents. A probe with both teams omniscient verified this over
\vsevenOmniProbeGames{} mirror games, in every one of which the non-moving team's score equalled the
number of half-suits dealt to it outright (\filepath{research/v06/notes/R8-game-structure.md}), so a
clairvoyant rollout assigns nearly the same value to every action available at a decision. This is
an implementation-level result verified by that probe, not a formal value bound, and it concerns
the evaluated variant. Determinization, the standard recipe
for hidden-hand card games \cite{levy,ginsberg-gib}, therefore reduces here largely to choosing the
ask most likely to hit, blind to the information an ask leaks, the information a refusal supplies,
and the option value of postponing a declaration. \S\ref{sec:related-determinization}
places this against the literature. Second, Fish has no passive continuation: a player must ask or
declare, so there is no equivalent of the check-and-call rollout on which the standard cheap
exploitability probe is built \cite{lisy2017lbr}. \S\ref{sec:discussion-future} names this as the
largest single obstacle between this work and a defensible near-optimality claim. Third, a
deterministic procedure reading only the public state and its own hand can be evaluated by a
teammate on any hypothesised hand; \S\ref{sec:agent-coordination} states the conditions under which
that supports coordination without communication.
%% --- end sections_v07/02-game ---
%% --- begin sections_v07/03-related ---
\section{Related work}
\label{sec:related}

We review computational work on Canadian Fish and related methods for hidden-state inference,
imperfect-information search, team coordination, and agent evaluation. For each group, we
distinguish methods used in this study from alternatives requiring assumptions or computational
resources not adopted here.

\subsection{Prior work on Canadian Fish}
\label{sec:related-fish}

The published material on this game is human and informal, and neither of its two substantial
sources is quantitative or describes a program. McLeod's Pagat entry \cite{pagat} is the canonical
rules source and records several points on which published variants differ; ours is specified in
\S\ref{sec:game-dialect}. Develin's chapter \cite{develin} is the most detailed strategy writing we
located, describes the same variant, and supplies two observations this design uses: an ask teaches
the opponents as much as it teaches a teammate, and a player is better off not declaring a half-suit
it holds entirely, since holding one carries no risk. The second is a tactic and not a rule: the
engine imposes no such condition, and \S\ref{sec:game-rules} states what it does impose. Secondary strategy pages \cite{dorsa,strategy-site,wikiliterature} restate blackballing,
asking the asker, and an endgame delegation heuristic; they are evidence about how the game is
played and talked about, not sources of quantitative claims.

\paragraph{Computational prior work.} The prior-art survey of 21 August 2026 queried the arXiv
API (\texttt{all:"Canadian Fish"}, \texttt{all:"Literature card game"}, \texttt{abs:"Go Fish"},
\texttt{all:"half-suit"}), GitHub code search (\texttt{literature card game}, \texttt{canadian fish
card game}, \texttt{fish card game ai}, \texttt{half suit card game}), the RLCard environment list
\cite{rlcard} and the Google Play store, and was refreshed on 22 and 23 August 2026 with topic
searches on search and inference in imperfect-information team games; the records are
\filepath{research/v04/lit/fish-prior-art.md}, \filepath{research/v05/lit/v05-refresh.md} and
\filepath{research/v06/notes/R7-literature-search.md}. Within that scope we did not identify
academic work on the six-player game, nor a public repository containing search, planning or a
working learner for it. The one agent identified, Somani's \texttt{literature}
\cite{somani,somani-server}, plays the four-player, 48-card variant, which does not raise the
six-player allocation problem, and publishes no strength numbers; a closed-source mobile
application advertises bots with no stated methodology \cite{playstore}. Every novelty statement in
this paper therefore takes the form ``we did not identify \ldots{}'' and is a statement about those
searches rather than a demonstrated absence. The Valet testbed
\cite{goadrich2026valet} packages twenty-one traditional imperfect-information card games in a
common description language with measured branching factors and durations; a shared testbed of
that kind would supply the external calibration this line of work lacks, but no comparison against
it is attempted here.

\subsection{Determinization and perfect-information analysis}
\label{sec:related-determinization}

Determinization, or perfect-information Monte Carlo (sample hidden cards consistently with the
public history, solve the resulting perfect-information game, and play the move with the best average
value), is the standard recipe for hidden-hand card games \cite{levy,ginsberg-gib}, and is
unsound however many worlds are drawn. Frank and Basin named the two mechanisms: \emph{strategy
fusion}, in which the searcher plays a different strategy in each sampled world although a real
player must commit to one strategy per information set, and \emph{non-locality}, in which a node's
value depends on parts of the tree outside its own subtree
\cite{frank-basin-ai,frank-basin-aaai}. Long, Sturtevant, Buro and Furtak characterise when the
method nevertheless performs well, through leaf correlation, bias and a disambiguation factor
\cite{long-pimc}; Information Set Monte Carlo Tree Search replaces independent trees with a single
tree over information sets \cite{cowling-ismcts,goodman-ris}; the $\alpha\mu$ family backs up Pareto
fronts of world-vectors instead of scalar averages, repairing both mechanisms, and reports Bridge
3NT declarer play at 60.2\% to 63.0\% with twenty worlds \cite{cazenave2019alphamu}; and
knowledge-based paranoia search reasons about the worst case over deduced-consistent worlds rather
than the average, reporting over 1{,}000 points on the extended Seeger scale in Skat
\cite{edelkamp2021paranoia}.

The evaluated variant presents an additional limitation for clairvoyant rollout evaluation, stated
in \S\ref{sec:game-why}: under clairvoyant play the available asks are scarcely distinguished in
value, so repairing strategy fusion and non-locality, as $\alpha\mu$ does, would not by itself make
a clairvoyant rollout informative here. We have not tested $\alpha\mu$ or its relatives on this
game, and the statement is about the evaluated variant rather than about those methods.

What this corpus adds is that the binding problem is not the abstract failure but \emph{the
statistic}. One determinization's value is the final half-suit differential, whose spread is an
order of magnitude larger than the differences between the leading candidates, so an argmax over
sampled means selects on residual noise, and does so more confidently the more candidates it is
offered. That is the optimiser's curse rather than strategy fusion, and \S\ref{sec:background-vsix}
reports it measured at \vsixCurseGap{}~pp by a control that forces the same code to return the
blueprint's own pick. These experiments motivate the guarded action-selection rule in
\S\ref{sec:agent-search}.

\subsection{Search under imperfect information}
\label{sec:related-search}

States in an imperfect-information game do not have well-defined values, so depth-limited search
needs something at the leaf that represents the opponent's ability to choose. Brown, Sandholm and
Amos supply it: let the opponent select among $k$ continuation strategies at the depth limit, each
yielding its own leaf-value vector, and require the searcher to be good against that choice
\cite{brown2018depthlimited}. The soundness argument is stated for two-player zero-sum games;
Fish's team-level game presents three opponent seats with distinct private information at the
leaf, and the cited two-player continuation construction was not instantiated for this setting, so
nothing is inherited. The idea is adopted here only as a robustification heuristic with a
measurable consequence, audited rather than assumed.

Two lines address what a search may prune. Zhang and Sandholm solve subgames over low-order
knowledge rather than the full common-knowledge closure, and prove that the naive form can
\emph{increase} exploitability \cite{zhang2021subgamesolvingwithoutck}. That warning applies directly
to the live-ask gate this policy family ships, which is a knowledge-limited pruning rule, and it is
why the side-channel and pathology gates of \S\ref{sec:evaluation-gate} were built before any
search work rather than after it. Subgame solving in adversarial team games builds a gadget over the
team belief DAG and runs in polynomial time given a best-response oracle under a sparsity condition
on the blueprint \cite{zhang2022teamsubgame}; the cited sparsity condition is not established for
the blueprint used here, and we did not attempt the construction.

In cooperative games the constraint transfers verbatim. SPARTA improves a blueprint purely at test
time and distinguishes single-agent search, which assumes every other agent strictly follows the
blueprint, from multi-agent search, in which all agents run the \emph{same} common-knowledge
procedure \cite{lerer2020sparta}. The moment a second teammate also searches, the first teammate's
belief over its partner is computed under a policy that partner is no longer playing, and the
improvement guarantee is void. Fish satisfies the requirement cheaply, for the reason
\S\ref{sec:game-why} gives; the qualifier about randomisation is what
\S\ref{sec:background-vsix} turns into an engine switch.

The full-solver line \cite{moravcik2017deepstack,brown2020rebel,schmid2023sog} established that
test-time re-solving over public belief states is supported by the formal models and assumptions in
those studies. We do not implement these algorithms: continual re-solving and ReBeL typically
iterate over, and Student of Games learns a value function on, a public belief state whose support
here is the set of deals consistent with the public history, of cardinality
$45!/(9!)^5 \approx 1.9 \times 10^{28}$ from one seat at deal time, and we did not attempt an
abstraction that would make that tractable for a six-player team game. Update equivalence recasts search as replicating a last-iterate-convergent update locally,
needing no public-information enumeration, and reports substantially lower search time than
public-belief-state search in Hanabi \cite{sokota2024updateequivalence}; its improvement guarantee
is stated for common-payoff games, which Fish is not. Learned Belief Search \cite{hu2021lbs} learns
a belief model for settings in which the exact belief is unavailable; we do not use it because the
constraint-conditioned belief is computed exactly here and the behavioural weighting is deliberately
simple, not because a learned belief could not add information. Online planning by policy
fine-tuning \cite{fickinger2021rlfinetuning} requires gradient updates at decision time and was not
attempted in this CPU-only engine with no network. The depth-limited counter-strategy line
\cite{milec2021cdr,milec2025abd} carries one warning this study honours, robust-response methods
do not compose with limited-look-ahead solving off the shelf, and the learned-model and
gadget-refinement work \cite{kubicek2025lamir,kubicek2026refinements} is recorded for its
meta-lesson rather than its machinery: when a search subproblem has many near-equivalent optima,
which one is selected is not a tie-break. That is the same observation \S\ref{sec:background-vsix}
makes about an argmax resolved by array order.

The result that a search of this kind can \emph{increase} exploitability is Zhang and Sandholm's
\cite{zhang2021subgamesolvingwithoutck}. Where the other work above carries a guarantee at all, its
conditions and this game's fit to them are recorded above, method by method. What none of them
supports is reading a win rate as a bound on exploitability, which is why this paper measures the two
separately. Local best response gives an approximate lower bound on best-response value: a small greedy
search looking one action ahead and assuming checks thereafter showed each of the 2016 competition
entrants it evaluated to be more than 3{,}180\,mBB/h exploitable \cite{lisy2017lbr}, the
competition's winning submission was not among them, and the authors say why. Its two documented
pitfalls are carried into \S\ref{sec:results-adversarial}: restricting where the response may begin
deviating understates exploitability, and a negative score proves nothing. Accordingly every
adversarial-response figure in this paper is reported as the observed win-rate edge of a particular
response within its class, which is a lower bound on the best-response value over that class only up
to sampling error, and never as an upper bound.

\subsection{Policy-aware inference}
\label{sec:related-inference}

Skat engines enumerate the deals consistent with the public record and reweight them by a model of
what each player would have done, a supervised per-card location model
\cite{solinas-supervised}, or reach probabilities taken from a policy \cite{rebstock-inference}, and a dedicated inference module was reported worth $+217$ tournament points per 36 games to the
engine Kermit on its own \cite{buro-kermit}. The policy prior of \S\ref{sec:inference-prior} is the
same idea in a much simpler form: a fitted soft reweighting of the rules posterior, with two
parameters and no learned opponent model.

This is the literature that has measured the same trade-off, and it is where
\S\ref{sec:background-vsix}'s belief-model comparison belongs. The finding there is that in this
game the constraint-conditioned distribution is computable exactly by a tractable factorised
dynamic program and is cheap, so in this implementation computational cost is not the only
motivation for approximation, and yet substituting the exact constraint-conditioned distribution
into an otherwise identical policy loses to the approximation with behavioural weighting, on two
independent seed banks.

Two adjacent lines are named. Belief modelling in cooperative games, the Bayesian Action Decoder's
public-belief formulation \cite{foerster2019bad} and its factorised-prescription successor
\cite{sokota2021capi}, is aimed at games whose exact belief is intractable, which is not the
situation here. The permanent-like counts that a constraint system of this shape generally induces
are the subject of exact formulae, Ryser's inclusion--exclusion formula \cite{ryser} and Glynn's
sign-vector formula \cite{glynn}, and of approximation algorithms, the Jerrum--Sinclair--Vigoda
chain \cite{jsv}, matrix scaling \cite{lsw} and sequential importance sampling \cite{chen-sis}; the
block structure of \S\ref{sec:inference} makes the exact count tractable directly, so none of the
five is needed for it. Matrix scaling appears in the deployed path for a different reason: the
approximate belief replaces the exact count join with a Sinkhorn iteration of that kind, not because
the exact count is unaffordable but to incorporate the two behavioural weighting parameters
(\S\ref{sec:inference-prior}). No learned belief model appears anywhere in this corpus; that is a
stated non-goal of the project.

\subsection{Team games and common information}
\label{sec:related-team}

The formal statement of three teammates sharing a payoff, holding different information and unable
to communicate is the common-information approach \cite{nayyar2013common}, discussed in
\S\ref{sec:game-why}. The team-game equilibrium literature is cited to delimit scope: the target
concept for a Fish team is a team-maxmin equilibrium with correlation \cite{celli-gatti}, connected
to extensive-form correlation by Farina and coauthors \cite{farina-collusion} and made computable
through the team belief DAG and the two-player conversion \cite{zhang-tbdag,carminati-tpi}, with
subsequent work on hidden-role settings \cite{carminati2024hiddenrole}. None of it is computed here.
We have not established whether a Fish team satisfies the recall conditions those constructions
require; the evident obstacle is that each seat privately observes nine cards its teammates do not.

The cooperative-play line is cited for its evaluation discipline rather than its algorithms. Hanabi
was proposed as a benchmark precisely because self-play scores overstate coordination
\cite{bard-hanabi}, and the ad-hoc-teamplay results that followed
\cite{hu-sad,hu-otherplay,hu2021obl,hu2022sed,jacob2022pikl,hu2022humanregularized,dizdarevic2025ah2ac2}
are the reason the cross-play measurement of \S\ref{sec:results-crossplay} exists at all: a
convention that collapses when partners are swapped is brittleness, and it is invisible to self-play
evaluation. Two neighbouring results are cited for what they show about porting rather than as
ad-hoc teamplay: joint policy search is the bridge-bidding analogue, scoring simultaneous changes at
several information sets \cite{tian2020jps}, and the Hanabi construction that most clearly fails to
port is the hat-guessing code \cite{cox-hanabi}, which works because each receiver sees every other
receiver's hand, and in Fish no player sees any other player's hand. The same lesson from a different direction is Cicero's use of a human-regularised policy
\cite{fair2022cicero}.

\subsection{Evaluation methodology}
\label{sec:related-eval}

The evaluation framework of \S\ref{sec:evaluation} draws on duplicate-match and
variance-reduction methods. Every cell in this paper is a paired duplicate rather than an
independent sample, on the model of duplicate bridge and of the Annual Computer Poker Competition's
duplicate matches and common seeds \cite{acpc}, whose protocols are also the model for a fixed
opponent panel scored identically for every arm. The estimator-side alternatives, DIVAT \cite{zinkevich-divat} and AIVAT
\cite{burch-aivat}, subtract a learned or hand-built value function from a single sample instead;
neither is used here, and the pairing is what makes the design competitive with them without
introducing a fitted object. Abstraction pathology
\cite{waugh-abstraction} is the standing warning that a model can be improved on its own metric and
made worse in play; \S\ref{sec:development-leaf} illustrates a related mismatch between a surrogate
metric and playing performance. Re-evaluation of agent
populations \cite{balduzzi-reeval} and the rating literature
\cite{coulom-whr,herbrich-trueskill,nelo,nelo-fishtest} inform why this paper reports a worst case and a minimax
regret over a shared panel rather than a single rating number. Recent work on gold-standard
evaluation practice \cite{kelidari2026goldstandard} is consistent with the preregistration and
sealing discipline of \S\ref{sec:evaluation-seal}, which this project adopted independently.
%% --- end sections_v07/03-related ---
%% --- begin sections_v07/04-background ---
\section{FishBot development background}
\label{sec:background}

\sestina{} is the seventh configuration in a lineage, and several of its components and most of its
evaluation machinery exist because of what earlier versions found. This section is deliberately
short. Its purpose is to explain why \sestina{} looks the way it does, not to re-report earlier
studies; the detailed corrections those studies require are collected in Appendix~\ref{app:corrections}.

\subsection{Engine, inference, and policy foundations (v0.2--v0.4)}
\label{sec:background-early}

The first versions established the domain and the inference. A count-conditioned, empirically tuned
linear policy in a scripting-language simulator became a C++ engine implementing the specified
rules and carrying an exact constraint-conditioned reference distribution, a faster deployed
approximation, a twenty-feature ask score and a value-based declaration rule. The durable
contribution of that period is the inference object of \S\ref{sec:inference}: an exactly computable
observer-conditioned constraint distribution over deals, cheap enough to run in all six seats at
once.

Earlier evaluations used smaller samples, unpaired ablations, and point-estimate comparisons: cells
were a few thousand games against a handful of opponents.

\subsection{Deadlock mitigation (v0.5)}
\label{sec:background-vfive}

FishBot \vfive{} addressed a mirror-play deadlock in which two agents repeatedly requested
unavailable cards until a forced declaration ended the sequence. Removing this behaviour did not
produce a statistically resolved win-rate improvement in the reported evaluation. The result
motivated separate checks of behavioural validity and playing strength, as well as reassessment of
the evaluation's precision.

\subsection{Policy and evaluation revisions (v0.6)}
\label{sec:background-vsix}

The v0.6 cycle set out to recover several points of headroom that the accumulated design register
described. It found little of it, and found in the process that much of the measurement machinery
lacked the precision its conclusions required. Four results from that cycle bear directly on
\sestina{}.

\paragraph{Tied action scores.} Measured in the \vfive{} mirror, the policy that cycle set out to diagnose, \vsixTieShareFive\% of
\emph{contested} ask decisions end with two or more candidates scoring bit-for-bit identically, and
the winner is whichever the enumerator emitted first. \vsixTieSameSuitFive\% of those ties are two
cards of one half-suit at one target, and the exact posterior \emph{separates} them in
\vsixTieExactSepPctFive\% of cases, meaning it assigns them equal probability in every case. The
same measurement on the \vsix{} mirror gives \vsevenTieContested\%, and that is the figure
\S\ref{sec:agent-tiebreak} and \S\ref{sec:development-tie} use, because \sestina{} modifies
\vsix{} rather than \vfive{}. Every rule that ranks tied candidates by that probability therefore
realises the same hit rate, and so does enumeration order. The largest item on the inherited design
register was not a defect but a property of the information set. What remains addressable is the
dependence on enumeration order itself, which is a correctness problem, and
\S\ref{sec:agent-tiebreak} is how \sestina{} addresses it.

\paragraph{Belief-model comparison.} Scored as predictors of card location on identical states in
the \vfive{} mirror, the ranking is:

\begin{center}\tabfont
\begin{tabular}{lrr}
\toprule
Posterior & Log loss (nats) & Argmax hit rate \\
\midrule
Exact, uniform deal prior & \vsixBeliefExactNLL{} & \vsixBeliefExactHit\% \\
Approximation, certificate half of the prior deleted ($\theta = 0$) & \vsixBeliefNoPriorNLL{} & \vsixBeliefNoPriorHit\% \\
\textbf{\vfive{}'s deployed approximation} & \textbf{\vsixBeliefShippedNLL{}} & \textbf{\vsixBeliefShippedHit\%} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The exact constraint-based model has the highest log loss among the compared models, and it
remains highest with the behavioural weighting deleted outright as well ($\theta = \varphi = 0$:
\vsevenBeliefPriorlessNLL{} nats and \vsevenBeliefPriorlessHit\%), so most of the log-loss gap has
no behavioural content, though most of the hit-rate gap does. The certificate half of the weighting
is the gap between the second and third rows; the weighting as a whole is the gap from the
$\theta = \varphi = 0$ figures to the third. In the implementation, the block construction of
\S\ref{sec:inference} has no parameter through which an opponent's tendencies could enter; the
behavioural weighting enters only through the deployed paths, as the initialiser of the Sinkhorn fit
(\S\ref{sec:inference-prior}) and, in the search, as an importance weight on exact samples
(\S\ref{sec:agent-search}). Whether an exact constraint computation could be combined with a
behavioural model in one object is not addressed here. This closed an
experiment three studies had listed as outstanding, and it established a methodological rule the
project has applied since, namely that exact constraint-based inference is an assumption to be
tested rather than a property to be assumed. \S\ref{sec:development-alloc} reports the second
comparison of that kind.

\paragraph{Search-selection bias.} v0.6
built a search that samples full deals from the public posterior and plays each forward with all six
players reconstructed at their own information sets, rather than made clairvoyant, which
\S\ref{sec:game-why} explains would leave the rollout unable to discriminate between actions. Chosen by an unguarded argmax over sampled means,
that search is \vsixCurseGap{}~pp \emph{worse} than the blueprint it searches from, measured against a
control that forces the same code to return the blueprint's own choice at identical budget, seed and
sample size. The cause is the statistic rather than the search (\S\ref{sec:agent-search}), and a
paired lower-confidence-bound deviation rule removed the measured deficit in that experiment. The guarded search, which v0.6 measured and then deployed switched off, is a component
of \sestina{} (\S\ref{sec:agent-search}).

\paragraph{Evaluation resolution and optimiser design.} Pooling every same-pair cell in the v0.6 battery gave \vsixHeadPooled\% with a naive $z$ of
\vsixHeadZ{}, a null that an earlier draft of that report published as one. Re-run at high power
across \vsixBigFiveBanks{} disjoint banks, the same comparison is \vsixBigFive\% over
\vsixBigFiveN{} games $[\vsixBigFiveLo, \vsixBigFiveHi]$, above parity on all \vsixBigFiveAbove{}
banks. The effect had always been present; the instrument could not see it. In the same cycle, five
mechanisms cleared a 95\% paired interval at the per-cell budget the project had used since v0.3 and
returned exactly zero at three times that budget. Separately, the fitting harness was found to be
incapable of moving a policy at all, using one step size for thirty-four coordinates spanning two and
a half orders of magnitude, an unpaired objective, and an objective documented as a worst case that
was in fact a weighted mean.

\subsection{Development objectives}
\label{sec:background-handoff}

The v0.6 cycle left a policy roughly nine tenths of a point stronger than its predecessor, a design
register most of whose entries had not produced a resolved gain, a repaired optimiser, and the open
question of whether, at the budget the project had used, the absence of resolved gains reflected
the policy class or the precision of the instrument.

The v0.6 results motivated five development stages: evaluation calibration, adversary generation,
candidate-mechanism testing, configuration selection and freezing, and holdout evaluation under a
preregistered protocol. \S\ref{sec:development} and \S\ref{sec:results} report the exploratory and
holdout stages, respectively. The protocol separated configuration selection from final evaluation
to reduce selection-related bias; the software processes and information restrictions that separated
the stages are stated in \S\ref{sec:evaluation-seal}.
%% --- end sections_v07/04-background ---
%% --- begin sections_v07/05-inference ---
\section{Exact constraint-conditioned deal inference}
\label{sec:inference}

Every agent in this lineage rests on an inference object that this section defines formally. The
method uses exact enumeration through a factorised dynamic program. The object it computes is the
uniform distribution over deals satisfying the acting player's hand information and the hard
constraints induced by the public history; \S\ref{sec:inference-prior} distinguishes that object
from a policy-aware posterior and describes the approximation the agent deploys.

\subsection{Notation}
\label{sec:inference-notation}

Table~\ref{tab:notation} fixes the symbols used here and in \S\ref{sec:agent}.

\begin{table}[!t]
\centering
\caption{Notation used in \S\ref{sec:inference} and \S\ref{sec:agent}.}
\label{tab:notation}
\tabfont
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}l L@{}}
\toprule
Symbol & Meaning \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\itshape The game and the hidden state} \\
$N=6$, $M=9$, $|H|=6$ & seats, half-suits, cards per half-suit; the deck is $M|H|=54$ cards \\
$s$ & the acting seat; $\mathcal{T}_s$ its two teammates, $\mathcal{O}_s$ its three opponents \\
$D$ & the hidden state: the initial deal, an assignment of all 54 cards to seats, nine each \\
$\mathcal{I}_s^t$ & seat $s$'s information set after $t$ public events \\
$\mathcal{C}_s^t$ & the hard constraints (C1)--(C5) seat $s$ has accumulated after $t$ events \\
$\mathcal{P}_t$ & a partial digest (rolling hash) of the public event stream (\S\ref{sec:agent-tiebreak}) \\
$\mathrm{own}_t(c)$, $H(c)$ & the seat holding card $c$; the half-suit containing $c$ \\
$\mathcal{H}_t$ & the half-suits not yet claimed \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\itshape Beliefs} \\
$\mathcal{M}_t(c)$ & the candidate set of card $c$: seats not yet excluded by a certificate \\
$U_t$, $n_p$ & the unresolved cards; the unresolved cards seat $p$ holds \\
$B_h$, $\mathcal{F}_h$ & the block of half-suit $h$; its assignments satisfying every certificate \\
$\sigma(\alpha)$, $T_h(\mathbf{v})$ & an assignment's per-seat count signature; the block count table \\
$F_k$, $G_k$ & the forward and backward partial convolutions of the count join \\
$q_s^t(D)$, $Z$ & the constraint-conditioned distribution of \eqref{eq:posterior}; the number of
  consistent deals \\
$\mu(c,p)$ & the exact marginal of $q_s^t$: the probability that seat $p$ holds card $c$ \\
$\hat\mu(c,p)$ & the deployed approximation to $\mu(c,p)$, with behavioural weighting \\
$\theta$, $\varphi$ & the two behavioural-weighting parameters of \eqref{eq:prior} \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{2}{@{}l}{\itshape Acting} \\
$\mathcal{A}_t$, $a=(c,q)$ & the legal ask set; an ask naming card $c$ and opponent $q$ \\
$p_a$ & $\hat\mu(c,q)$, the modelled hit probability of ask $a$ \\
$\phi(a)$, $w$, $u(a)$ & the ask feature vector, its fitted weights, and the resulting score \\
$\lambda$, $\beta$ & the linear and value scale parameters of \eqref{eq:askscore} \\
$M_H$, $q_H$ & opponent-held mass and residual ambiguity in half-suit $H$, in \eqref{eq:contest} \\
$\mathcal{E}_t$, $\varepsilon$ & the bit-for-bit tied candidate set and its tolerance \\
$\pi_{\mathrm{alloc}}$, $\pi_{\mathrm{team}}$ & under $q_s^t$: the probability a named allocation
  is exactly correct; that the team owns a half-suit outright \\
$\hat\pi_{\mathrm{alloc}}$, $\hat\pi_{\mathrm{team}}$ & the deployed approximations to those two
  quantities (\S\ref{sec:agent-declare}) \\
$\delta$, $\tau$ & the declaration value margin and the forced-endgame threshold \\
$\chi_t$, $\nu_t$, $K$ & the deduction-state digest, the no-progress counter, its threshold \\
$\Phi_t$ & the potential function of the termination argument in \S\ref{sec:agent-stall} \\
$D_{\mathrm{det}}$, $w_d$ & the number of sampled worlds and their importance weights \\
$\kappa$, $\eta$ & the search's deviation threshold and its blueprint blend, $\eta = 0$ here \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\subsection{Engine architecture and information access}
\label{sec:inference-engine}

The engine is a single-header C++20 core. A hand is one 64-bit word over the 54 card indices
$c = 6h + i$ for half-suit $h$ and offset $i$, so hand arithmetic is bit arithmetic. Random streams
are counter-based and keyed by the triple (seed, seat, purpose). This gives the property the
duplicate-block design of \S\ref{sec:evaluation-duplicate} requires: a deal is reproducible from
its seed, and the play of a deal between deterministic agents reproduces from the seed and the
agents' configurations. Complete reproducibility of a scored run is a stronger property, and
\S\ref{sec:agent-coordination} and Appendix~\ref{app:corrections-self} record where searching
agents fall short of it.

An acting policy never receives the game state. It receives its own knowledge object, its own hand
and the public event stream. There is no reference from a policy to the deal, to another seat's hand
or to the referee. This is an architectural barrier rather than a convention, and it is what allows
the side-channel audit of \S\ref{sec:evaluation-side} to be read as a test of the policy: a policy
that cannot reach the deal cannot read it, so a replay failure indicates dependence on information
or state not represented in the replay construction. Subsequent source inspection localised the
observed residual to persistent rollout state (\S\ref{sec:agent-coordination}), although the
specific carrier field and its scored win-rate effect were not isolated.

\subsection{Observer-specific deal constraints}
\label{sec:inference-constraints}

Because a card changes hands only through an observed ask, the public record determines
$\mathrm{own}_t(\cdot)$ given $D$. The only hidden object is therefore $D$ itself. Write
$\mathcal{D}$ for the set of all deals; from one seat's viewpoint at deal time
$|\mathcal{D}| = 45!/(9!)^5 \approx 1.9 \times 10^{28}$.

Each public event imposes a \emph{hard} constraint, meaning a logical restriction on $\mathcal{D}$
rather than a probabilistic tilt. The engine maintains them as a per-card candidate set
$\mathcal{M}_t(c)$, initialised to all six seats. For every card that remains unresolved the set
only shrinks, $\mathcal{M}_{t+1}(c) \subseteq \mathcal{M}_t(c)$; a card whose holder becomes public
through a transfer leaves the unresolved set $U_t$ with its holder recorded, so the monotonicity
statement is about unresolved cards:

\begin{description}
\item[(C1) Own hand.] For each $c$ in seat $s$'s hand, $\mathcal{M}(c) \leftarrow \{s\}$; for every
  other card, $s$ is removed from $\mathcal{M}(c)$.
\item[(C2) Card counts.] Every seat's current hand size is public, so with the transfer record each
  seat's count of unresolved cards, $n_p$, is known. This is the only constraint that couples
  different half-suits.
\item[(C3) Transfers.] A successful ask makes $\mathrm{own}(c)$ public from then on, so
  $\mathcal{M}(c)$ collapses to a singleton.
\item[(C4) Failed asks.] If $q$ was asked for $c$ at time $r$ and did not hold it, then $q \notin
  \mathcal{M}(c)$ unless $q$ has acquired $c$ since, which is public.
\item[(C5) Ask legality.] An asker of $c$ held another card of $H(c)$ at that moment and did not hold
  $c$. The first is a disjunction over the other five cards of the half-suit and is the one
  constraint that is not a simple exclusion.
\end{description}

Write $\mathcal{C}_s^t$ for the constraints seat $s$ has accumulated. The object this section
computes is the uniform distribution over the deals that satisfy them:
\begin{equation}
  q_s^t(D) \;=\; \frac{\ind\!\left[D \models \mathcal{C}_s^t\right]}{Z},
  \qquad
  Z \;=\; \bigl|\{D' \in \mathcal{D} : D' \models \mathcal{C}_s^t\}\bigr|.
  \label{eq:posterior}
\end{equation}
This distribution is exact for the stated constraint model. It is not, in general, the posterior
$\Pr[D \mid \mathcal{I}_s^t]$: a uniform prior over initial deals does not imply a uniform posterior
after strategically chosen actions, because different feasible deals can make the observed action
sequence differently likely under the players' policies. A policy-aware posterior would weight each
feasible deal by the likelihood of the observed actions under a specified behavioural model, which
is the computation the transcript-inversion adversary of \S\ref{sec:results-whitebox} approximates
and \eqref{eq:coord} states. The modelling content of \eqref{eq:posterior} is entirely in which
constraints are imposed; nothing in it refers to how anyone plays. \S\ref{sec:inference-prior}
describes the behavioural weighting the deployed approximation adds.

\subsection{Half-suit factorisation and count dynamic programming}
\label{sec:inference-factor}

Computing $Z$ by enumerating $\mathcal{D}$ is infeasible, and a constraint system of this shape
generally induces a permanent-like count. For such counts the literature supplies exact formulae
that are exponential in the general case, inclusion--exclusion \cite{ryser} and the sign-vector
formula \cite{glynn}, and approximation algorithms, Markov chains \cite{jsv}, matrix scaling
\cite{lsw} and sequential importance sampling \cite{chen-sis}.

Fish avoids that, because every constraint except (C2) is confined to a single half-suit. Constraints
(C3), (C4) and (C5) all speak about one card or one half-suit; only the per-seat capacities $n_p$
couple half-suits, and they couple them only through counts.

Partition the unresolved cards by half-suit, $U_t = \bigcup_{h} B_h$, where $B_h$ is the
\emph{block} of half-suit $h$. For a block, let an \emph{assignment} be a map $\alpha: B_h \to
\{0,\dots,5\}$ with $\alpha(c) \in \mathcal{M}(c)$, and let $\mathcal{F}_h$ be the assignments that
additionally satisfy every (C5) disjunction touching $h$. Write the \emph{count signature} of an
assignment as the vector
\[
  \sigma(\alpha) \;=\; \bigl(|\alpha^{-1}(0)|,\;\dots,\;|\alpha^{-1}(5)|\bigr) \in \mathbb{Z}_{\ge 0}^{6}.
\]

\paragraph{Block enumeration.} For each half-suit independently, enumerate $\mathcal{F}_h$
and accumulate a table indexed by count signature,
\begin{equation}
  T_h(\mathbf{v}) \;=\; \bigl|\{\alpha \in \mathcal{F}_h : \sigma(\alpha) = \mathbf{v}\}\bigr|,
  \qquad
  T_h^{c \to p}(\mathbf{v}) \;=\; \bigl|\{\alpha \in \mathcal{F}_h : \sigma(\alpha) = \mathbf{v},\,
  \alpha(c) = p\}\bigr|.
  \label{eq:blocktable}
\end{equation}
A half-suit holds six cards and resolved ones are removed, so $|B_h| \le 6$ and the enumeration is
small.

\paragraph{Count convolution.} Constraint (C2) says the signatures must sum to the vector of
per-seat capacities $\mathbf{n} = (n_0,\dots,n_5)$. Order the blocks arbitrarily as $h_1, \dots,
h_M$ and define the partial convolution. A half-suit carrying no live (C5) disjunction factorises
further into one independent unit per card, so the implementation runs one layer per unit rather
than one per half-suit; the recursion below is the same object either way.
\begin{equation}
  F_0(\mathbf{v}) = \ind[\mathbf{v} = \mathbf{0}], \qquad
  F_k(\mathbf{v}) \;=\; \sum_{\mathbf{u}} F_{k-1}(\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{u})\, T_{h_k}(\mathbf{u}),
  \label{eq:dp}
\end{equation}
with every table taken as zero outside the box $\mathbf{0} \le \mathbf{v} \le \mathbf{n}$
(componentwise), so that
\begin{equation}
  Z \;=\; F_M(\mathbf{n}).
  \label{eq:Z}
\end{equation}
By induction on $k$, $F_k(\mathbf{v})$ counts the joint assignments of the first $k$ blocks that
satisfy their own certificates and have total signature $\mathbf{v}$; since the blocks partition
$U_t$ and (C2) is the only constraint that couples them, $F_M(\mathbf{n})$ counts exactly the
assignments satisfying (C1)--(C5), which is $Z$. Running the same recursion backwards, with
$G_{M+1}(\mathbf{v}) = \ind[\mathbf{v} = \mathbf{0}]$ and $G_k(\mathbf{v}) = \sum_{\mathbf{u}}
T_{h_k}(\mathbf{u})\, G_{k+1}(\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{u})$, gives suffix tables $G_k$, and the marginals
follow by substituting one block's card-resolved table for its plain one:
\begin{equation}
  \mu(c,p) \;=\; \frac{1}{Z}\sum_{\mathbf{u},\,\mathbf{v}}
  F_{k-1}(\mathbf{v})\; T_{h_k}^{c \to p}(\mathbf{u})\; G_{k+1}(\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{u}),
  \qquad c \in B_{h_k}.
  \label{eq:marg}
\end{equation}

Three quantities the policy needs are \emph{defined} by this machinery, and
\S\ref{sec:inference-prior} records that the deployed configuration substitutes a cheaper
approximation for each of them. The per-card marginals are \eqref{eq:marg}. The probability under
$q_s^t$ that the acting team owns half-suit $h$ outright is the total weight of the block
assignments whose image lies in the team,
\begin{equation}
  \pi_{\mathrm{team}}(h) \;=\; \frac{1}{Z}
  \sum_{\substack{\alpha \in \mathcal{F}_h\\ \alpha(B_h) \subseteq \mathcal{T}_s \cup \{s\}}}
  \;\sum_{\mathbf{v}} F_{k-1}(\mathbf{v})\; G_{k+1}\bigl(\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{v}-\sigma(\alpha)\bigr),
  \qquad h = h_k.
  \label{eq:team}
\end{equation}
And the probability of a fully named allocation, which is exactly what a declaration asserts, is
\begin{equation}
  \pi_{\mathrm{alloc}}(h, \alpha^\star) \;=\;
  \frac{\ind\!\left[\alpha^\star \in \mathcal{F}_h\right]}{Z}
  \sum_{\mathbf{u}, \mathbf{v}} F_{k-1}(\mathbf{v})\,
  \ind\!\left[\sigma(\alpha^\star) = \mathbf{u}\right]
  G_{k+1}(\mathbf{n}-\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{u}),
  \label{eq:alloc}
\end{equation}
for the single assignment $\alpha^\star$ the declaration names. The leading indicator is not
cosmetic: an assignment with a surviving count signature need not itself satisfy the half-suit's
certificates, so feasibility is checked explicitly. No sampling is involved in any of the three.

\paragraph{Cost.} The signature space is bounded by the capacities, each block table has at most
$6^{|B_h|}$ entries before pruning, and the join is $M$ convolutions over that space. An exact
rebuild was timed at a mean of \vsixCountCost{}~ms in the \vsix{} cycle's measurement, taken over
the exact-program builds at contested ask decisions in 150 mirror self-play games on the desktop
described in Appendix~\ref{app:repro}; the cost is strongly state-dependent and the figure is a
mixed-regime mean. The counts are exact as a formulation; the implementation evaluates the block
tables in single precision and the convolutions, $Z$ and the marginals in double precision with no
rescaling, so the values are floating-point evaluations of exact integer quantities, and $Z$ at deal
time exceeds the range in which a double represents integers exactly. The verified calculation is the constraint-conditioned count and
its marginals; it is not a policy-aware posterior, and the project's decision to spend its cycles on
policy rests on the former being cheap rather than on inference being solved in the latter sense.

\subsection{Approximate inference and behavioural weighting}
\label{sec:inference-prior}

Equation~\eqref{eq:posterior} is exact \emph{for the constraint model}: it is the uniform
distribution over constraint-consistent deals. It contains no model of behaviour, because the
construction above consumes the deduction state alone and has no parameter through which an
opponent's tendencies could enter.

The path the agent deploys adds two such parameters and gives up exactness for them. The
history-dependent weighting it introduces is called the \emph{policy prior} in the code and in
earlier reports; this paper calls it a behavioural weighting model, since it is a reweighting of the
constraint-consistent deals by the observed ask history rather than a prior in the Bayesian sense.
It replaces
the count join by iterative proportional fitting, better known here as Sinkhorn scaling, on the
$|U_t| \times 6$ matrix of admissible card--seat pairs. Initialise
\begin{equation}
  \hat\mu^{(0)}(c,p) \;=\; \ind\!\left[p \in \mathcal{M}(c)\right] \cdot
  \exp\Bigl(\operatorname{clip}\bigl(\theta\, a_{p,H(c)} - \varphi\, (A_p - a_{p,H(c)}),\; \pm 2.6\bigr)\Bigr),
  \label{eq:prior}
\end{equation}
where $a_{p,h}$ is the number of asks seat $p$ has made in half-suit $h$ and $A_p$ its total asks, and
then alternate the two normalisations
\begin{equation}
  \hat\mu(c,\cdot) \leftarrow \frac{\hat\mu(c,\cdot)}{\sum_{p}\hat\mu(c,p)},
  \qquad
  \hat\mu(\cdot,p) \leftarrow \hat\mu(\cdot,p)\cdot\frac{n_p}{\sum_{c}\hat\mu(c,p)},
  \label{eq:sinkhorn}
\end{equation}
to a fixed iteration count. The row step makes each card's distribution a probability; the column
step enforces the capacity constraint (C2) that the exact join enforces combinatorially.

Between outer sweeps the routine also applies an independence conditioning step for each live (C5)
disjunction: for a certificate saying that seat $j$ held \emph{some} card of a half-suit,
each candidate card $c'$ of that half-suit is rescaled to
$\hat\mu(c',j) \big/ \bigl(1 - \prod_{c''}(1 - \hat\mu(c'',j))\bigr)$, the product running over the
half-suit's cards $c''$.
So (C5) is neither enforced exactly nor discarded in the deployed path: it is imposed approximately,
under an independence assumption the exact enumeration of \S\ref{sec:inference-factor} does not need.
The deployed parameters are $\theta = \vsevenPriorTheta{}$ and $\varphi = \vsevenPriorPhi{}$.

Equation~\eqref{eq:prior} is the entire behavioural content of the agent's beliefs: a seat that has
asked repeatedly in a half-suit is treated as more likely to hold cards of it, because asking
requires already holding one. It is a soft version of the (C5) certificate, in the spirit of the Skat
inference line \cite{solinas-supervised,rebstock-inference,buro-kermit} but far simpler, with two
parameters and no learned opponent model.

\S\ref{sec:background-vsix} reports the model comparison: scored as predictors of card location on
identical states, $\hat\mu$ has lower log loss and a higher argmax hit rate than $\mu$. In this
implementation, exactness for the constraint model and behavioural weighting are in different
objects: the block program carries no behavioural parameter, and the deployed paths add the
weighting either as the initialiser of the Sinkhorn fit or, in the search, as an importance weight
on exact samples (\S\ref{sec:agent-search}). In the comparison, the weighting contributed more
predictive accuracy than exactness did. That is why the
paper treats exact constraint-based inference as an assumption to test rather than a property to
assume; \S\ref{sec:development-alloc} reports a second comparison of the same kind.

\subsection{Implementation verification}
\label{sec:inference-verify}

Three checks run over the engine, and all three ran as preconditions of the evaluation in
\S\ref{sec:results}.

\begin{description}
\item[Soundness audit.] Every seat's candidate set is checked against the true deal at every
  decision: $\mathrm{own}_t(c) \in \mathcal{M}_t(c)$ must hold always, since no agent's deduction may
  exclude the truth. The verification run for this evaluation recorded
  \vsevenVerifyViolations{} violations in \vsevenVerifyChecks{} checks, and the preceding cycle
  \vsixAuditViolations{} in \vsixAuditChecks{}.
\item[Cross-engine agreement.] The block dynamic program, an independent card-counting path and an
  exact rejection sampler must agree on \eqref{eq:marg}. The self-test reports
  \vsevenSelftestChecks{} checks passing, a check being one (card, observer, event) row. Its
  tolerances are $10^{-9}$ for block-versus-card-program agreement, which is checked on states with
  no live (C5) disjunction and holds there at the limit of double precision, and $0.05$ for
  block-versus-rejection-sampling agreement on states with live disjunctions, which is Monte Carlo
  precision at the sample sizes the test draws. A separate brute-force oracle checks $Z$, the
  marginals, and the team and allocation probabilities against exhaustive enumeration on small
  states to $10^{-9}$.
\item[Identity control.] The current policy, carrying its predecessor's parameter vector with every
  new mechanism disabled, must produce a transcript identical to its predecessor's under md5. It does
  (\vsixIdentity{}). This control checks whether refactoring changes behaviour before the new
  components are enabled, so that a measured ablation difference is not an artefact of refactoring.
\end{description}

Two defects these checks found are recorded here because both changed previously published numbers,
and both survived for the same reason: they were invisible to the path the agent actually deploys.
The block dynamic program parked every instance's tables in a shared per-thread pool, so a second
construction on the same thread silently repointed the first instance's tables, giving
\numAliasMismatch{} mismatches in \numAliasChecks{} checks; any earlier number computed with the
exact belief in a configuration where more than one seat held such an object is affected, and the
deployed approximate-belief numbers are not. Separately, an audit that verifies the declaration
pre-gate never rejects a correct declaration had been parsed only in an older branch and so had never
run; once enabled it reports \vsixGateFalseNeg{} false negatives over \vsixGateOpportunities{}
declaration opportunities. Appendix~\ref{app:corrections} carries the full register.
%% --- end sections_v07/05-inference ---
%% --- begin sections_v07/06-agent ---
\section{\sestina{} architecture}
\label{sec:agent}

\sestina{} is a configuration of the \vsix{} policy family rather than a new architecture. This
section describes the agent conceptually: Figure~\ref{fig:pipeline} shows the decision
pipeline, and Table~\ref{tab:components} sets its components against \vsix{}'s. The literal
configuration string, its option map, the parameter vector layout and the freeze artifact's hashes
are in Appendix~\ref{app:parameters}.

\subsection{Action space and ask policy}
\label{sec:agent-policy}

At its turn a seat faces one decision with two branches: declare a half-suit, or ask. Declaration is
treated first in the code and is described in \S\ref{sec:agent-declare}; this subsection covers the
ask.

\paragraph{The legal action set.} With $\mathcal{O}_s$ the opponents and $\mathcal{H}_t$ the
half-suits not yet claimed, the rules of \S\ref{sec:game-rules} give
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal{A}_t \;=\; \bigl\{(c,q) \;:\; q \in \mathcal{O}_s,\; \text{hand}_t(q) \neq \emptyset,\;
  H(c) \in \mathcal{H}_t,\; c \notin \text{hand}_t(s),\;
  \text{hand}_t(s) \cap H(c) \neq \emptyset \bigr\}.
  \label{eq:legal}
\end{equation}
The last condition encodes the ownership condition that makes an ask informative: the asker must
already hold a card of the half-suit, so asking publishes partial ownership.

What the policy actually enumerates is a strict subset of \eqref{eq:legal}. A \emph{live-ask filter} first removes candidates the seat can already prove are misses, that is,
those whose target is excluded by a certificate, $q \notin \mathcal{M}(c)$. It is this filter, rather
than any weighting inside \eqref{eq:askscore}, that keeps a provably unsuccessful ask off the score. It
binds: it removes what would otherwise have been the argmax at roughly \vsevenGateBindRate\% of
\vsevenGateBindN{} ask decisions captured from \sestina{}'s own seats. If no candidate survives the filter the full legal set is
restored, so the guarantee is not absolute.

\paragraph{The score.} Each candidate is scored by a fitted linear function of a
$20$-dimensional feature vector $\phi(a)$, plus a learned value term and, in \sestina{}, three
additional ownership terms:
\begin{equation}
  u(a) \;=\; \lambda\, w^{\!\top}\phi(a) \;+\; \beta\, V_{\mathrm{ask}}(a)
  \;+\; \lambda\bigl(w_{\mathrm{void}}\, g_1(a) + w_{\mathrm{team}}\, g_2(a)
  + w_{\mathrm{last}}\, g_3(a)\bigr),
  \label{eq:askscore}
\end{equation}
with $\lambda = \vsevenLinearWeight{}$ and $\beta$ scale parameters of the fit. \sestina{} adds a
fourth block of \vsevenResponderTotal{} extended coordinates, of which
\vsevenResponderNonzero{} is non-zero; that one is \eqref{eq:contest} and
\S\ref{sec:agent-contest} gives it. Writing $p_a = \hat\mu(c,q)$ for the modelled
hit probability, the features $\phi$ are functions of the belief and the public state. They include $p_a$ and $p_a^2$; an indicator that the card is publicly
certain; the acting seat's holding in $H(c)$ and the posterior mass its team already carries; the
best continuation probability elsewhere in the half-suit; the
reply threat and exposure a miss would create, both weighted by $1-p_a$; the target's hand size and
an indicator that this ask would empty it; the binary entropy $\mathrm{H}_2(p_a)$; and a score-margin
term. Appendix~\ref{app:parameters} lists all twenty with their fitted weights.

The three extra terms are the ones \vsix{} added, and each expresses an effect the twenty could not:
\begin{align}
  g_1(a) &= p_a \prod_{c' \in H(c) \setminus \{c\}} \bigl(1 - \hat\mu(c',q)\bigr),
    \label{eq:gvoid}\\
  g_2(a) &= \Pr\bigl[\,\mathrm{own}(c) \in \mathcal{T}_s \cup \{s\}\,\bigr],
    \label{eq:gteam}\\
  g_3(a) &= p_a \cdot \ind\bigl[\,q \text{ is the last opponent holding cards, and holds one}\,\bigr].
    \label{eq:glast}
\end{align}
Equation~\eqref{eq:gvoid} is an independence-based proxy for the probability that the ask takes
the target's \emph{last} card of the half-suit: it multiplies the marginals as if the target's
holdings of the other cards were independent, which the count-constrained distribution does not
imply. Equation~\eqref{eq:gteam} is the team-ownership probability of the named card, the belief
mass the acting team already carries, and \eqref{eq:glast} is an indicator that the ask would
trigger the forced endgame. Equation~\eqref{eq:gvoid} expresses the loss of the target's access to
that half-suit: because a legal ask requires holding a card of the half-suit, removing a target's
last card of $H(c)$ permanently removes that target's right to ask in it.

\paragraph{Selection.} The policy takes the argmax, with the tie handling of
\S\ref{sec:agent-tiebreak} and, when enabled, the search of \S\ref{sec:agent-search}:
\begin{equation}
  a^\star \;=\; \operatorname*{arg\,max}_{a \in \mathcal{A}_t} u(a).
  \label{eq:argmax}
\end{equation}

\sestina{} inherits $w$, $\lambda$, $\beta$ and the three extra weights from \vsix{} unchanged.
No gradient or population fit was run in this cycle: the one coordinate that is not
inherited, \eqref{eq:contest}, had its value chosen as the argmax of a measured dose response, which
is a one-dimensional selection on win rate and is reported as such in
\S\ref{sec:results-selection}. The vector was found by \vsix{}'s repaired
optimiser under a minimax-regret objective over a four-member opponent panel. Of the
\vsevenCoordinates{} coordinates in the extended vector a number are known to be inert, and
Appendix~\ref{app:parameters} lists which and why.

\subsection{Public-history tie-breaking}
\label{sec:agent-tiebreak}

In the \vsix{} mirror, \vsevenTieContested\% of contested ask decisions end with two or more
candidates scoring bit-for-bit identically under \eqref{eq:askscore}
(\S\ref{sec:background-vsix}), and the winner was whichever the enumerator emitted first, under an
unstable sort. That is a specification problem rather than a strength problem. The choice is
arbitrary, and it is determined by an implementation detail: the result of an unstable sort is
reproducible given the same implementation and inputs, but the policy does not specify it, so a
teammate modelling the seat would have to model the sort rather than the rule. The defensible
benefit of the rule below is a specified, reproducible selection rule that reads only the public
record.

Order the candidates by score and let the tied set be
\begin{equation}
  \mathcal{E}_t \;=\; \bigl\{a \in \mathcal{A}_t \;:\; u(a) \ge u(a_{(1)}) - \varepsilon \bigr\},
  \label{eq:tieset}
\end{equation}
where $a_{(1)}$ is the top-ranked candidate and $\varepsilon$ is a fixed tolerance. \sestina{}
replaces enumeration order with a deterministic draw keyed by the public record:
\begin{equation}
  a^\star \;=\; \mathcal{E}_t\bigl[\,\iota(\rho,\,|\mathcal{E}_t|)\,\bigr],
  \qquad
  \rho \;=\; \mathrm{hash}\bigl(\mathcal{P}_t,\; s,\; t\bigr),
  \label{eq:tiebreak}
\end{equation}
with $\mathcal{P}_t$ a partial digest of the public event stream, namely a rolling hash over each
event's kind, actor, target, card and outcome, which omits the half-suit index and the allocation a
declaration names; whether those omitted public fields can matter to a tie case was not
established. Here $s$ is the seat index, $t$ the event count, and $\iota$ a uniform index map from
the hash into the tied set. The tolerance
$\varepsilon$ is $10^{-9}$ in absolute score units, a fixed engine constant rather than a
configuration option, so $\mathcal{E}_t$ is a tolerance-based tied set rather than necessarily a
bit-identical one. Every seat observes all three arguments of $\rho$, so every teammate can
recompute it; no seat's private hand enters it. The index is applied to the tied block in the order
the score sort leaves it, and the arrangement of equal keys under that sort is fixed by the
implementation rather than by the rule, so what \eqref{eq:tiebreak} specifies is the index into the
block; an identical build reproduces the arrangement, as the isolation audit of
\S\ref{sec:results-controls} confirms for every non-searching decision.

Two scope conditions belong with that. \eqref{eq:tiebreak} governs the non-search path only: when
the endgame search fires it supplies its own selection (\S\ref{sec:agent-search}) and index $0$ is
whatever the sort left first. And the search draws its determinizations from a private per-seat
stream seeded from the harness, so the policy is a deterministic function of
$(\text{own hand}, \text{public record}, \text{reset seed})$ rather than of the first two alone. That
third argument is exactly what the side-channel audit of \S\ref{sec:evaluation-side} replays, which
is why the audit is a test of seat isolation and not of determinism.

The engine also offers a variant that draws $\rho$ from a private per-seat stream. That variant is
\emph{not} used: it would break the reconstructibility that makes \eqref{eq:tiebreak} a coordination
device. \S\ref{sec:development-tie} reports what happened when a later experiment tried to
\emph{select} within $\mathcal{E}_t$ rather than randomise over it.

\subsection{Half-suit contestation}
\label{sec:agent-contest}

The component with the largest observed leave-one-out drop in the holdout ablations
(\S\ref{sec:results-attribution}) is a single additional coordinate, one non-zero entry out of
\vsevenResponderTotal{} in an extended block of the parameter vector. Let $M_H$ be the expected number of cards of half-suit $H$ held by the
opposing team, and $q_H$ the number whose holder this seat cannot place. The term is
\begin{equation}
  r(a) \;=\; (1 - p_a)\cdot\frac{M_{H(c)}}{6}\cdot\frac{q_{H(c)}}{6},
  \label{eq:contest}
\end{equation}
entering \eqref{eq:askscore} with a \vsevenContestSign{} coefficient of
\texttt{\vsevenContestWeight{}}, so a large value \emph{encourages} the ask. It selects asks that are
