likely to miss, into half-suits the opponents dominate, whose ownership is still ambiguous.

\paragraph{Feature interpretation.} The feature was designed to price \emph{information denial}: a
failed ask publishes a negative certificate, and the intent was that the fitted coefficient would
come out negative, meaning refuse to hand that certificate over. It came out positive. The negative
branch was swept separately and is flat to negative throughout, reaching \vsevenContestNegDose{}~pp
at the mirror-image dose, so the measured dose response does not support the design intent.

What the positive branch does was measured on the target rather than inferred. Over
$\sim$\vsevenContestDecisions{} target ask decisions, the target's ask accuracy falls from
\vsevenContestTargetAskBefore{} to \vsevenContestTargetAskAfter{}, while its declaration accuracy
\emph{rises} and it becomes \emph{less} often urgent, which is inconsistent with a declaration-channel
mechanism, where this phase expected to find the effect. The reading these measurements are
consistent with is contestation: asking into half-suits the opponents are trying to complete
degrades the quality of their asks, and the acting team pays part of the same cost, its own ask
accuracy falling from \vsevenContestSelfAskBefore{} to \vsevenContestSelfAskAfter{}. Three sibling
coordinates written to express the same denial idea were measured at the same dose and are inert or
harmful, which is consistent with a benefit specific to this feature combination in the tested
ablations rather than to opponent mass in general; a positive coefficient and the associated changes
in opponent accuracy suggest a mechanism without establishing it as the unique explanation.

Two facts about its provenance matter for \S\ref{sec:results-attribution}. It was found by a
coordinate sweep during adversary generation rather than by architecture work, and it is hand-set:
the value \texttt{\vsevenContestWeight{}} is the argmax of a dose response measured at
\vsevenPhaseTwoCell{} games a dose, not the output of a gradient or population fit. And it is not
uniformly good. Against the then-deployed policy it scored \vsevenOneSwitchArm{}~pp, but its worst
cell over the \vsix{} frontier is \vsevenOneSwitchArmWorst{}~pp against
\texttt{\vsevenOneSwitchArmWorstOpp{}}, which is negative, so it is a strength gain with a worst case
rather than an exploit (\S\ref{sec:development-adversaries}).

\subsection{Declaration policy}
\label{sec:agent-declare}

A declaration names, for each of the six cards of a half-suit, which member of the declaring team
holds it. It is all-or-nothing: correct, and the team scores the half-suit; wrong, and the opponents
do. The decision therefore has two parts, choosing what to claim and choosing when.

\paragraph{Allocation selection.} For half-suit $h$ the reference objective is the most probable
allocation among the acting team under the constraint-conditioned distribution,
\begin{equation}
  \alpha^\star(h) \;=\; \operatorname*{arg\,max}_{\alpha:\,H \to \mathcal{T}_s \cup \{s\}}
  \; \pi_{\mathrm{alloc}}(h,\alpha),
  \label{eq:bestalloc}
\end{equation}
with $\pi_{\mathrm{alloc}}$ from \eqref{eq:alloc}. Since \eqref{eq:alloc} depends on
$\alpha^\star$ only through its count signature, the argmax in \eqref{eq:bestalloc} is generally
not unique; the reference implementation takes the lexicographically first feasible assignment
among the maximisers. Equation~\eqref{eq:bestalloc} defines the reference objective; the deployed
approximation is described here. Three procedures must be kept
distinct. \emph{Marginal-product selection}: \sestina{} enumerates the capacity-feasible
assignments of the unresolved cards of $h$ to its own team and takes the argmax of the product of
per-card approximate marginals, $\prod_{c} \hat\mu(c,\alpha(c))$; that product is the selection
score only. \emph{Sequential approximate joint scoring}: the selected allocation is then priced
separately, by sequential conditioning, in which the belief is refit after each card of the
allocation is fixed in turn, and the resulting chain-rule product is what the policy reports as
$\hat\pi^\star_{\mathrm{alloc}}$ and thresholds on. \emph{Exact constraint-joint selection}: the
exact object \eqref{eq:alloc} is available from the block dynamic program but sits on no decision
path. \S\ref{sec:development-alloc} reports the relevant measurements: the marginal-product
selection and the sequential approximate joint's argmax selected the same allocation in every one
of \vsevenLoneDeclsTotal{} recorded voluntary declarations, of which \vsevenLoneDeclsDistinct{} are
from distinct (bank, urgency-setting) capture cells and the remainder from re-runs of the same
seeds and from the two cells of the sequential-joint policy, an agreement that is empirical and
does not establish equivalence to the exact joint maximiser; and substituting the exact constraint-joint selection
changed a small fraction of decisions and performed worse on the declaration decision.

\paragraph{Declaration timing.} Write $\hat\pi_{\mathrm{alloc}}^\star$ for the sequentially
estimated probability of the selected allocation and $\hat\pi_{\mathrm{team}}$ for the deployed
estimate of the probability the team owns $h$ outright, which is the larger of
$\hat\pi_{\mathrm{alloc}}^\star$ and the product over the half-suit's unresolved cards of the team's
summed marginals, $\prod_{c}\sum_{p \in \mathcal{T}_s \cup \{s\}}\hat\mu(c,p)$, an independence
product rather than \eqref{eq:team}. The value function is written on the
assumption that waiting is free, on the ground that an opponent cannot claim a half-suit the acting
team provably holds. That ground binds strictly only in the locked case, and the rule is applied
down to a team-ownership probability well below one, so the assumption is an approximation rather
than an identity. Under it the decision is an approximate comparison between immediate declaration
value and the value of waiting: declaring scores a point now with probability
$\hat\pi_{\mathrm{alloc}}^\star$, and publishes the team's holdings either way. The agent evaluates
that with its learned state-value function $V$:
\begin{equation}
  \Delta \;=\; \underbrace{\hat\pi_{\mathrm{alloc}}^\star\, V(\text{state} \mid \text{correct})
  \;+\; (1-\hat\pi_{\mathrm{alloc}}^\star)\, V(\text{state} \mid \text{wrong})}_{\text{declare}}
  \;-\; \underbrace{V(\text{state})}_{\text{wait}},
  \label{eq:declev}
\end{equation}
and declares when $\Delta > \delta$ for a fitted margin $\delta$. Both conditioned states differ from the waiting state in having the \emph{unresolved} cards of $h$
removed from the unresolved set, typically fewer than six, and in the deltas of the four
half-suit-control features the value function reads, all of which are functions of the team's
expected share of $h$ before the claim. They differ from \emph{each other} only in the score margin,
by $+1$ and $-1$.

A gate precedes \eqref{eq:declev}: a half-suit is priced by the value rule only if its deployed
team-ownership estimate $\hat\pi_{\mathrm{team}}$ clears a fitted floor, which is lowered under
pressure stage 1 and removed under stage 2. Two overrides sit above \eqref{eq:declev}. Under the
forced endgame, when the opposing team is cardless and the acting team must resolve the remaining
half-suits among itself, the engine polls the team down the willingness ladder of
\S\ref{sec:game-dialect}, and at each level the agent is willing to declare a half-suit when
$\hat\pi_{\mathrm{alloc}}^\star \ge \tau$ for that level's threshold $\tau$; the final level is
compulsory. Under \emph{pressure}, the escalation described next, stage 1 lowers the thresholds,
declaring when $\hat\pi_{\mathrm{alloc}}^\star \ge 0.5$, and stage 2 removes them: the best
available allocation is declared unconditionally, whatever its probability.
\sestina{} removes the mechanism that used to raise pressure in ordinary play, and
\S\ref{sec:agent-stall} is what now supplies stage 2.

\subsection{Urgency-escalation removal}
\label{sec:agent-urgency}

\vsix{} inherited a declaration-urgency escalation keyed to an event counter. Past a threshold of 220
public events the declaration rule lowers its team-ownership floor and permits declarations with
estimated correctness probabilities near 0.5, which costs \vsevenCliff{}~pp to a policy held in that
regime. No adversary
reaches it, since the longest game any adversarial search produced against an unmodified opponent is
\vsevenCliffIncumbentTail{} events, but the machinery is defective in ordinary play as well:
\S\ref{sec:development-alloc} reports that removing it eliminates \vsevenLoneUrgencyShare\% of the
entire declaration allocation-error class. \sestina{} disables it
(\texttt{pool}, \texttt{oppfloor}, \texttt{force}, \texttt{askfloor}).

The escalation was, however, also the policy's termination safeguard, so removing this mechanism
requires an alternative stall safeguard.

\subsection{Deduction-state stall detection}
\label{sec:agent-stall}

The escalation \sestina{} removes was keyed to a clock. The condition the clock stood in for is
that the monitored deduction state has not changed, and that condition is directly observable from
the seat's own deduction state.

Let $\chi_t = \mathrm{hash}\bigl(\mathrm{own}_t(\cdot),\, \mathcal{M}_t(\cdot),\, U_t,\,
(\text{hand sizes})_t,\, (\text{live half-suits})_t\bigr)$, the digest of the hard certificates the
seat holds. It deliberately \emph{excludes} the list of outstanding (C5) disjunctions: a repeated ask
appends a duplicate disjunction carrying no information, so counting that list as progress would
blind the detector to the one loop it exists to break. Define the
no-progress counter
\begin{equation}
  \nu_t \;=\;
  \begin{cases}
    0, & \chi_t \neq \chi_{t-1},\\
    \nu_{t-1} + 1, & \chi_t = \chi_{t-1},
  \end{cases}
  \label{eq:stall}
\end{equation}
and escalate to pressure stage 1 when $\nu_t \ge K$ and to stage 2 when $\nu_t \ge 2K$, with
$K = \texttt{\vsevenOptStallK{}}$ and $2K = \texttt{\vsevenOptStallTwo{}}$ in \sestina{}.

\paragraph{Scope of the termination safeguard.} Consider
$\Phi_t = \sum_{c \in U_t}\bigl(|\mathcal{M}_t(c)| - 1\bigr) + |U_t| + |\mathcal{H}_t|$. Certificates
only accumulate: a seat excluded from a card's candidate set is never restored, a resolved card is
never unresolved again, and a claimed half-suit never returns. Every event that changes $\chi$ by one
of those routes therefore strictly decreases $\Phi_t$, which is a non-negative integer, so there can be
only finitely many of them. This potential-function argument does not establish termination,
because transfers can change the monitored state without decreasing the proposed potential: the
digest also contains hand sizes, so a successful ask always counts as progress, and a card moving
between two seats is not a monotone gain; nothing above forbids a card being won back later. The rule therefore bounds the mode
of non-termination that actually occurs, a run of asks producing no new certificate, and not every
mode. That is why this paper calls it a safeguard, and why \S\ref{sec:discussion-engineering} records
termination as empirical.

The threshold was set from measurement. Over \vsevenStallMirrorGames{} mirror games \vsix{} never
produces a no-progress run longer than \vsevenStallIncumbentRun{}, while the deliberately deadlocked
negative control of \S\ref{sec:evaluation-gate} produces \vsevenStallFrozenRun{}. Any
$K \in [12,60]$ was therefore not reached in the sampled ordinary games and was reached in the
tested deadlock control, after $K$ and then $2K$ no-progress events. Armed, the rule's mirror
transcript digest is byte identical to the same configuration without it: in those mirror games its
effect on play is identically zero, because $\nu_t$ never reaches $K$.
\S\ref{sec:results-attribution} confirms that on holdout material. Note also that $\nu_t$ is a
function of the seat's own hand and the public record alone, so the rule opens no information
channel.

\subsection{Guarded determinized search}
\label{sec:agent-search}

\sestina{} enables the search \vsix{} built, measured, and then deployed switched off. It runs only
in the endgame, when at most \texttt{\vsevenOptMaxq{}} cards remain unresolved, and it revises the
blueprint's choice only when a standard-error-penalised paired comparison favours the revision.

\paragraph{Deal sampling.} The search draws $D_{\mathrm{det}} = \texttt{\vsevenOptDet{}}$
determinizations from the constraint-conditioned distribution \eqref{eq:posterior}, using a
per-card capacity dynamic program as a uniform sampler over deals satisfying (C1)--(C4), with the
(C5) disjunctions enforced by rejection, so an accepted draw is an exact sample from
\eqref{eq:posterior}. This sampler is a distinct object from the block program of \S\ref{sec:inference-factor},
which enumerates (C5) inside its blocks instead. $D_{\mathrm{det}}$ is a target rather than a
guarantee: draws are attempted up to a cap and the rule proceeds with however many survive
rejection. If no draw survives, or the sampler cannot be built, the decision falls back to the
\vfive{} scoring path rather than to the blueprint's own choice; that path scores with the
twenty-feature vector alone and breaks ties in enumeration order. No artifact of this cycle
records how often the fallback fired for the frozen configuration. The behavioural weighting of \eqref{eq:prior} is then applied as an
importance weight rather than baked into the sampler, which keeps the sampler exact and the
weighting auditable:
\begin{equation}
  \tilde w_d \;=\; \prod_{c \in U_t} \exp\Bigl(\operatorname{clip}\bigl(\theta\, a_{o_d(c),H(c)}
  - \varphi\,(A_{o_d(c)} - a_{o_d(c),H(c)}),\, \pm 2.6\bigr)\Bigr),
  \qquad
  w_d = \frac{\tilde w_d}{\sum_{d'} \tilde w_{d'}},
  \label{eq:iw}
\end{equation}
where $o_d(c)$ is the seat holding card $c$ in world $d$.

\paragraph{Rollout evaluation.} The candidate set is the top $\texttt{\vsevenOptCand{}}$ blueprint
candidates by score, extended through any tie at the cut up to a cap of twenty, with index $0$
reserved for the blueprint's own choice $a_{(1)}$, the first candidate after the score sort rather
than the output of \eqref{eq:tiebreak}. For each candidate $r$ and world $d$, all six seats are reconstructed \emph{at their own information
sets} under $d$, each playing a cheaper blueprint than the acting agent's own: the \vfive{} policy
with an independent-marginal belief and no re-scoring pass and the game is played to a terminal state or until \texttt{\vsevenOptDepth{}} further public events
have elapsed. A run reaching the end returns the acting team's half-suit differential; a run cut at the limit
returns a \emph{material} evaluation of the position instead, being the scored half-suit differential
plus the team's expected control of the live half-suits. That is \vsix{}'s own hand-written leaf, not
a learned one: \S\ref{sec:development-leaf} reports what happened when a fitted replacement was
built. Write $V_{r,d}$ for either. This is the point at which the method departs from standard
determinization: the continuation players are not made clairvoyant. \S\ref{sec:game-why} explains why
that matters here, namely that under clairvoyant play the implemented rules give the team on turn
every half-suit not dealt outright to the opponents, so a clairvoyant rollout assigns nearly the
same value to every action. All candidates share the same $D_{\mathrm{det}}$ worlds, so the comparison is paired and the
noise that matters is the noise in the difference.

\paragraph{Guarded action selection.} For each candidate $r \ge 1$ form the paired difference
against the blueprint,
\begin{equation}
  m_r \;=\; \sum_{d} w_d\,\bigl(V_{r,d} - V_{0,d}\bigr),
  \qquad
  \mathrm{var}_r \;=\; \sum_{d} w_d\,\bigl(V_{r,d} - V_{0,d} - m_r\bigr)^2,
  \label{eq:paired}
\end{equation}
and with the Kish effective sample size $n_{\mathrm{eff}} = 1/\sum_d w_d^2$ take
\begin{equation}
  \mathrm{se}_r = \sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{var}_r}{n_{\mathrm{eff}} - 1}},
  \qquad
  \mathrm{LCB}_r = m_r - \kappa_r\,\mathrm{se}_r + \eta\,\bigl(u(a_{(r+1)}) - u(a_{(1)})\bigr).
  \label{eq:lcb}
\end{equation}
The search deviates to $\arg\max_r \mathrm{LCB}_r$ if that maximum is strictly positive, and
otherwise plays the blueprint's choice. When the effective sample size is at most $1.5$, which
includes the case of a single surviving world or one dominant weight, $\mathrm{se}_r$ is set to an
effectively infinite value, so no candidate outside the tied set can deviate and within it the raw
weighted paired mean decides. The shrinkage is $\kappa = \texttt{\vsevenOptKappa{}}$. The term $\eta$ blends the blueprint's own
ranking back in and is \textbf{zero in this configuration}, so \eqref{eq:lcb} reduces to a pure
paired lower confidence bound.

Equation~\eqref{eq:lcb} is the action-selection guard used in the evaluated configuration. Chosen
instead by an unguarded $\arg\max_r m_r$, the same search is \vsixCurseGap{}~pp \emph{worse} than
the blueprint it searches from (\S\ref{sec:background-vsix}): one world's return is a final
half-suit differential whose spread is an order of magnitude larger than genuine differences between
candidates, so the maximum selects on residual noise, and does so more confidently the more
candidates it is offered. Requiring $\kappa$ standard errors of a \emph{paired} difference removed
that measured deficit in the \vsix{} experiment. The rule is a lower-confidence-bound heuristic
rather than a test with established finite-sample coverage: at most $D_{\mathrm{det}}$ weighted
worlds enter it, several candidates are compared against the same reference, and the effective
sample size can be small. What the record establishes is that the guard reduced the measured
deficit, not that it guarantees an improvement over the blueprint.

One detail is worth stating because \S\ref{sec:development-tie} turns on it. Within the tied set
$\mathcal{E}_t$ of \eqref{eq:tieset} the rule applies $\kappa_r = 0$, that is, no shrinkage at all,
which is precisely where its selection signal is most noise-dominated.

\subsection{Coordination and information constraints}
\label{sec:agent-coordination}

\sestina{} deploys as three identical agents. They hold different private hands, they may not
signal, and the only channels the rule variant leaves are the forced-endgame willingness bit and the
successor a cardless turn-holder names (\S\ref{sec:game-dialect}), for both of which
\S\ref{sec:game-dialect} reports no resolved effect from the tested interventions. The
coordination assumptions are therefore the following.

They rest on determinism plus a shared observation. Consider first the idealised non-searching
policy, and let $\Pi$ denote it: a map from $(\text{own hand}, \text{public record})$ to an action.
Every teammate runs the same $\Pi$ and observes the same public record $\mathcal{P}_t$. Teammate $j$ cannot compute $\Pi$'s output at seat
$i$, because it does not know $i$'s hand. But it knows $\Pi$ itself, and it knows $\mathcal{P}_t$, so
it can compute the \emph{distribution} of $i$'s action induced by its own posterior over $i$'s hand,
\begin{equation}
  \Pr\bigl[\,a_i = a \mid \mathcal{I}_j^t\,\bigr]
  \;=\; \sum_{D \,\models\, \mathcal{I}_j^t}
  \Pr\bigl[D \mid \mathcal{I}_j^t\bigr]\;
  \ind\!\left[\Pi\bigl(\text{hand}_i(D),\, \mathcal{P}_t\bigr) = a\right],
  \label{eq:coord}
\end{equation}
and, having observed $a_i$, condition on it. That is exactly the (C5) reasoning of
\S\ref{sec:inference-constraints} carried one step further: a legality certificate says the asker
held \emph{some} card of the half-suit, while \eqref{eq:coord} says the asker held a hand on which
$\Pi$ preferred that ask. The deployed approximation does not evaluate \eqref{eq:coord} in full,
which would require enumerating hands; it captures the first-order part of it through the ask-tally
prior \eqref{eq:prior}. The white-box adversary of \S\ref{sec:results-whitebox} is the construction
that approximates \eqref{eq:coord} against \sestina{} rather than for it: it evaluates the one-step
action likelihood on exact samples and accumulates per-card evidence additively rather than
carrying the joint conditioning across the game, and \S\ref{sec:development-instrument} reports
what that inversion is worth.

Two conditions make this work, and both are engineering constraints rather than assumptions. $\Pi$
must be \emph{specified}: a teammate reasoning about seat $i$ must be able to evaluate
$\Pi(\cdot,\mathcal{P}_t)$ on a hypothesised hand from the rule alone. An unstable sort does not
break determinism, since it is deterministic for a fixed input and build, but its result is fixed by
an implementation detail rather than by the policy specification, so a teammate would have to model
the implementation; \eqref{eq:tiebreak} replaces it with a specified rule on the non-search path.
And $\Pi$ must be common knowledge, which for three copies of one published agent it is. No shared
secret and no pre-play randomisation are used, which is the sense in which the team's coordination
is public (\S\ref{sec:threat}).

Equation~\eqref{eq:coord} is stated for the idealised non-searching policy. The deployed agent
departs from it in two ways. When the search of \S\ref{sec:agent-search} fires, the action also
depends on the seat's reset seed, which is private to the seat, so the deployed policy is a function
of $(\text{own hand}, \mathcal{P}_t, \text{reset seed})$ on that path (\S\ref{sec:agent-tiebreak});
a teammate can still compute the distribution of the action given a hypothesised hand only by
modelling that randomness. And the cross-deal state persistence described next introduces a further
dependence that neither a teammate nor \eqref{eq:coord} models.

\paragraph{Cross-deal state persistence.} Agents are constructed once per worker thread and
reused across the deals that thread is given, and some per-agent state survives the inter-deal reset,
reachable only under the search. This violates the premise that $\Pi$ depends on
$(\text{own hand}, \mathcal{P}_t)$ alone: a decision can depend on which deals the thread happened to
process earlier. The carrier was identified as the rollout engine, which the reset never touches; the
specific field was not. It was deliberately not repaired, because the one-line fix changes the play of
every searching configuration by roughly one deal in \vsevenSsixIncidence{}, and applying it after the
configuration was frozen would defeat the purpose of freezing.
\S\ref{sec:results-controls} measures its magnitude on holdout material, and
\S\ref{sec:discussion-engineering} states what it costs the argument above.

\subsection{Decision procedure}
\label{sec:agent-full}

Collecting the pieces, one turn of \sestina{} at seat $s$ and time $t$ runs as follows. The steps
describe the deployed approximate path, not the reference objects of \S\ref{sec:inference}.

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Update beliefs.} Fold the new public events into the candidate sets
  $\mathcal{M}_t(\cdot)$ by (C1)--(C5), then compute $\hat\mu$ by \eqref{eq:prior} and
  \eqref{eq:sinkhorn}. Update the no-progress counter $\nu_t$ by \eqref{eq:stall}.
\item \textbf{Consider declaring.} For each live half-suit passing the cheap ownership gates,
  enumerate the capacity-feasible allocations of its unresolved cards to the acting team and select
  the allocation maximising the product of approximate card marginals. Estimate its probability
  $\hat\pi_{\mathrm{alloc}}^\star$ by sequential conditioning and belief refitting. If the
  team-ownership estimate clears its floor, declare when $\Delta > \delta$ in \eqref{eq:declev};
  declare regardless if a pressure override fires, and answer the forced-endgame ladder by its
  thresholds (\S\ref{sec:agent-declare}).
\item \textbf{Otherwise ask.} Enumerate the legal set $\mathcal{A}_t$ by \eqref{eq:legal} and apply
  the live-ask filter of \S\ref{sec:agent-policy}, removing candidates whose target is excluded by a
  certificate; if the filter removes every candidate, restore the legal set. Score the resulting
  candidates by \eqref{eq:askscore}, including the contestation term \eqref{eq:contest}.
\item \textbf{Resolve ties.} Form $\mathcal{E}_t$ by \eqref{eq:tieset}. If the endgame condition does
  not hold, return \eqref{eq:tiebreak} and stop.
\item \textbf{Search.} Otherwise sample $D_{\mathrm{det}}$ worlds with weights \eqref{eq:iw}, roll out
  each candidate in each world, and return the candidate selected by \eqref{eq:lcb}. This is the
  blueprint's own choice, the first candidate after the score sort, unless a candidate's paired
  lower confidence bound is positive; the bound uses $\kappa_r = \kappa$ standard errors for
  candidates outside the tied set $\mathcal{E}_t$ and $\kappa_r = 0$ within it, and if no world
  survives rejection the \vfive{} scoring path decides (\S\ref{sec:agent-search}).
\end{enumerate}

Steps 1--4 read only the acting seat's own hand and the public record; step 5 also reads the seat's
own reset seed. \S\ref{sec:agent-coordination} records that, in the evaluated implementation, a
searching agent's decisions can additionally depend on state carried across deals, so the property
that every decision is a function of the listed information holds for the non-searching path and is
an approximation for the searching one. The side-channel audit of \S\ref{sec:evaluation-side} tests
the property mechanically rather than by inspection, and \S\ref{sec:results-controls} reports where
it fails.

%% --- end sections_v07/06-agent ---
%% --- begin sections_v07/07-evaluation ---
\section{Evaluation framework}
\label{sec:evaluation}

This section describes the evaluation protocol. Each component was adopted in response to a
specific measured failure in an earlier cycle, and \S\ref{sec:background-vsix} describes those
failures.

\subsection{Outcome measures}
\label{sec:evaluation-measure}

The primary outcome is the team win rate, the proportion of games in which the evaluated
configuration's team takes at least five of the nine half-suits. Comparisons are reported as an
\textbf{edge in percentage points},
\[
  \text{edge} \;=\; 100 \times (\text{win rate} - 0.5),
\]
so that zero denotes a 50\% win rate in the specified comparison and the sign gives the direction.
The paper writes \emph{pp} for percentage points of this quantity, and a bracketed pair
$[\ell, u]$ after an estimate is its two-sided 95\% confidence interval (CI) as constructed in
\S\ref{sec:evaluation-intervals}. A comparison ``against'' a named configuration is a head-to-head
comparison: an edge of $+3$~pp means a 53\% win rate against that configuration in the paired
design of \S\ref{sec:evaluation-duplicate}, not a three-point change from a separately measured
baseline win rate. Three other quantities appear and are never conflated with it: the mean number
of half-suits a team takes, used for the search's internal return; predictive log loss and argmax
hit rate, used to score belief models as predictors (\S\ref{sec:background-vsix}); and the
\emph{adversarial response win-rate edge} of \S\ref{sec:results-adversarial}, which is an edge in
the same units but measured for a fitted opponent against the evaluated configuration.

\subsection{Duplicate deal blocks}
\label{sec:evaluation-duplicate}

A \emph{cell} compares two configurations $A$ and $B$ over a \emph{bank} of deals identified by one
integer seed. Every deal in the bank is played twice: once with $A$ in the odd seats and $B$ in the
even seats, and once with the seats exchanged. Both configurations therefore play the identical deal
from both sides.

Fish has large per-deal variance, since a deal can be won or lost on the cards, and an unpaired
comparison spends most of its sample resolving that variance rather than the difference between
policies. The rotation balances team assignments within each deal and can reduce the variance associated
with deal strength, in the spirit of duplicate bridge and of the variance-reduction estimators
developed for poker \cite{zinkevich-divat,burch-aivat}. The design requires reproducibility from the seed alone
(\S\ref{sec:inference-engine}); without it the two rotations are not the same deal and the design
degrades silently into an unpaired one.

\subsection{Uncertainty estimation}
\label{sec:evaluation-intervals}

The two rotations of one deal are not independent observations: the two games share an initial deal
and are therefore treated as a single resampling block. Resampling blocks preserves their
within-deal dependence, whatever its sign.

Three interval constructions are used, and they are labelled by their construction rather than
described as one method.

\paragraph{Per-bank intervals.} Every cell measured on one bank carries a two-sided 95\%
  \textbf{deal-clustered percentile bootstrap interval}. The resampling unit is the \emph{deal},
  carrying both of its rotations together; 20{,}000 bootstrap resamples are drawn with replacement
  from the deals of the cell, the edge is recomputed on each resample, and the interval is the
  empirical 2.5th and 97.5th percentiles of that distribution. No Wilson or normal-approximation
  interval on the game count is quoted for a single bank. The harness records both intervals for
  every cell, and in this corpus the clustered interval is typically no wider than, and often
  slightly narrower than, the interval that treats games as independent, which is the direction a
  negative within-deal correlation produces.
\paragraph{Independence assumption.} All three constructions treat deal blocks as independent
  resampling units. In the scored implementation, agents are reused across the deals a worker
  thread processes and some rollout state persists between deals (\S\ref{sec:agent-coordination}),
  so a decision in one deal can depend on earlier deals and on thread scheduling, and the
  work-stealing schedule is not retained. The independence assumption is therefore violated for
  searching agents, and the coverage of every interval in this paper is nominal rather than
  established under the actual data-generating construction. The observed residual is small
  (\S\ref{sec:results-controls}), but its effect on coverage is not bounded by any measurement, and
  the prose qualification does not repair it; \S\ref{sec:discussion-engineering} states what
  would.
\paragraph{Cross-bank pooling.} Two banks of equal size pool as the mean of their two edges, with
  standard error $\mathrm{se}_{\text{pooled}} = \sqrt{\mathrm{se}_1^2 + \mathrm{se}_2^2}/2$, where
  each $\mathrm{se}$ is recovered from that bank's bootstrap interval as half-width divided by
  $1.96$. The pooled interval is the estimate plus or minus $1.96\,\mathrm{se}_{\text{pooled}}$. This
  is a normal approximation built on the per-bank bootstrap standard errors, not itself a bootstrap
  interval. Both per-bank values are printed beside every pooled figure in this paper.
\paragraph{Between-cell contrasts.} A difference between two separately measured cells, such as an
  ablation's leave-one-out drop, is the difference of the estimates with the two half-widths
  combined in quadrature, omitting the covariance between the two cells. The configurations are
  paired on deals, so the two cells' estimation errors are generally correlated; the omission
  overstates the width only when that covariance is non-negative, which is expected when both cells
  are measured on the same deals against the same opponent but is not verified cell by cell. The
  harness produces no paired difference \emph{across} two separately executed cells, and the
  scored artifacts record per-cell summaries rather than per-deal outcomes, so a joint bootstrap of
  the actual contrast would require re-running the cells with per-deal capture; that is listed in
  \S\ref{sec:discussion-future}. Where a contrast combines cells that
  share banks and deals with unit coefficients, as the rule-variant residual of
  \S\ref{sec:results-dialects} does, the propagated interval is not a valid 95\% interval and is
  labelled as such where it appears.

\subsection{Sample-size planning}
\label{sec:evaluation-power}

At parity, the 95\% half-width of a cell's edge in percentage points is approximately
$98/\sqrt{N}$ for $N$ games, the half-width of a normal interval for an independent Bernoulli mean
at $p = 0.5$. The harness computes this planning approximation for every cell and emits it with the
result. It treats the $N$ games as independent and ignores the duplicate-block covariance, so it is a
planning figure rather than a power calculation: at $N = \vsevenScoreboardGames{}$ it gives a
one-point half-width, which does not mean that a one-point effect would be detected with any
specified probability. No formal power calculation, with a specified test, effect size, significance
level, desired power and block-level variance, was performed. The arithmetic is applied in advance
because it determines what may be attempted: a one-point half-width requires roughly
\vsevenScoreboardGames{} games, and a quarter-point half-width requires sixteen times that.

The failure this planning step addresses is the one \S\ref{sec:background-vsix} describes, in which
five mechanisms cleared a 95\% paired interval at one budget and returned exactly zero at three
times it. Sample sizes in this paper are chosen from the arithmetic before a cell is run, never
after seeing its result.

\subsection{Prespecified sign-agreement criterion}
\label{sec:evaluation-replication}

Every claim is measured on two disjoint deal banks, and the criterion was fixed before the data were
seen:

\begin{quote}
A claim whose \textbf{sign} does not agree on both banks is reported as not replicated, whatever its
pooled interval says.
\end{quote}

This is stricter than a pooled significance test, deliberately. A pooled interval can exclude zero
while the two banks disagree, and that configuration has occurred in this corpus and has previously
been published as a positive result. The criterion binds against this paper's own findings in
\S\ref{sec:results-attribution} and again in \S\ref{sec:results-panel}, and both instances are
reported.

\subsection{Holdout sealing and preregistration}
\label{sec:evaluation-seal}

A programme that generates candidate configurations, scores them and selects the best is performing a
maximisation, and the selected configuration's score is upward biased by the amount a maximum over
$K$ draws exceeds the mean. No care taken in the final measurement removes that bias if the final
measurement uses material the selection has seen.

Before any candidate existed, deal banks were reserved by seed and their digests committed, a digest
being a rolling hash over the six dealt hands and the dealer of every deal in the bank, computed
without constructing a policy or playing a game. An adversary population was split in half by a rule
fixed in advance, and one half was encrypted with the plaintext's SHA-256 committed, so that it could
not be trained against even accidentally. The engine's match command enforces the seal: a run
naming a sealed seed fails unless an explicit environment variable is set, which was set once, at
the start of the final evaluation, and recorded. The guard's scope is the match command; the
fitting, ablation and probe drivers derive their own seeds and bypass it, as Appendix~\ref{app:deviations}
(D6) records, and a committed digest is a commitment to content rather than a proof that the content
was not accessed. The documented access procedure is the one recorded there.

The entire final battery, comprising every cell, its sample size, its threshold, the arithmetic
behind the sample size and the conditions that would count as non-confirmation, was written down and
committed before any holdout bank had been played and before the sealed half had been decoded. The
evaluation was then executed as a separate automated agent session: a large language model
operating the repository under the author's supervision, with the session settings recorded in
\filepath{docs/v07/PHASE-PROMPTS.md}, started without conversation history from the development
phases and instructed to read the registered protocol and not the development-phase documents or
training logs. That restriction was by instruction rather than by access control, since the session
had read access to the repository, and the same author configured every phase; no evaluator
independence is claimed. Where the evaluation required a training figure for comparison it took it
from the registered protocol, which states such expectations in advance for exactly this purpose, so that agreement
constitutes a check and disagreement constitutes a finding.

Anything the evaluation did that the protocol does not specify is recorded as a deviation, next to
the affected result. There are \vsevenDeviations{}, listed in Appendix~\ref{app:deviations}.

\subsection{Selection effects and multiple comparisons}
\label{sec:evaluation-multiplicity}

This study evaluates many candidate mechanisms, opponents, ablations, adversarial searches and
configurations, and the inferential status of that needs stating precisely.

No formal family-wise or false-discovery correction was applied, and no interval reported
here is adjusted for multiplicity. What the design provides instead is structural.
\emph{Registration} fixes the primary comparison, its threshold and its interpretation before the
holdout outcomes were examined, so the primary claim is a single pre-specified test rather than the
best of many.
\emph{Sealing} guarded the named holdout banks against routine match access and committed their
digests before any candidate existed; the guard is an environment-variable override on the match
runner, some fitting and diagnostic drivers bypass it, and a digest commits to content without
making it inaccessible (\S\ref{sec:evaluation-seal}, Appendix~\ref{app:deviations} D5 and D6), so
this is a guarded procedure with prior commitments rather than unavailability by construction. \emph{Disjoint banks with a sign criterion} require a claim to appear twice
before it is reported. \emph{Calibration} (\S\ref{sec:evaluation-floor}) supports the interpretation of a null
adversarial result under the registered responder class, budget, handicap family and banks; it does
not make every null interpretable. And an explicit
\emph{selection check} (\S\ref{sec:results-selection}) computes how much of the measured gain a
maximum over $K$ candidates would produce under the null and compares advance-stated training figures
against their holdout counterparts.

Exploratory comparisons in \S\ref{sec:development} are reported as exploratory: they were run on
training material during the development phase, none is treated as confirmatory, and none is used to
support the paper's primary claim.

\subsection{Behavioural validity checks}
\label{sec:evaluation-gate}

Win rate and behavioural validity are dissociable in this policy family. The corpus contains a
configuration that scores \vsevenScoreboardTrapWin\% against an earlier version while making
\vsevenScoreboardTrapDead\% provably unsuccessful asks, asks whose target it can already prove does
not hold the card (\S\ref{sec:agent-policy}), and losing \vsevenScoreboardTrapLimit\% of its games
to the action cap. It improves win rate against the specified opponent while failing the chosen
behavioural criteria.

A configuration is therefore gated \emph{before} any strength number is computed for it, on
\vsevenGateRulesWord{} rules covering provably unsuccessful asks, runs of them, action-limit games,
the mirror game-length tail, late declarations, and the side-channel tests below. The protocol calls
this the \emph{soundness gate}; outside quotations this paper calls it the behavioural validity
gate, because it is an empirical test suite encoding behavioural criteria, not a proof. A provably unsuccessful ask is legal and
could in principle serve an information or turn-transfer purpose, so a rejection expresses a design
requirement rather than the invalidity of every rejected strategy. A configuration that fails is
rejected regardless of its win rate. The gate runs on \emph{training} material by design, being a
check on self-play behaviour rather than a strength claim. It is accompanied by a negative control,
a configuration constructed to be rejected, because a gate that cannot reject it would establish
nothing about the configuration it accepts.

\subsection{Adversarial-search calibration}
\label{sec:evaluation-floor}

An adversarial-response figure is produced by a search, and a search that finds nothing admits two
explanations: there is nothing to find within the class and budget, or the search is too weak.
Planted-handicap experiments characterise sensitivity to selected failure modes. An artificial
handicap of known size is planted in a target, a responder is fitted against the handicapped
target, and the \emph{excess} of that responder's edge over its edge against the unhandicapped
target is measured. The smallest planted edge the procedure recovers, for a given search class,
budget and handicap family, is that class's \textbf{detection floor}.

Three quantities that this number is sometimes made to stand for are kept separate in this paper.
The first is the observed planted-handicap recovery threshold for a particular search class,
budget and handicap family, which is what the calibration measures. The second is the sampling
uncertainty of a fixed policy comparison, which is a property of the cell's sample size and is
described by its interval (\S\ref{sec:evaluation-intervals}), not by the floor. The third is the
preregistered decision threshold of \vsevenPrimaryFloor{}~pp for declaring an advance
(\S\ref{sec:design-protocol}), which the protocol set equal to the phase-2 calibration floor of the
principal responder class. The protocol itself describes that use as a pre-committed reference bar
rather than a detection floor for a directly measured paired difference; it is a historical
decision rule, not a resolution limit for every comparison in the paper.

The \vsevenPrimaryFloor{}~pp figure has a specific origin. The phase-2 calibration planted a ladder
of handicaps of increasing size in a \vsix{} target and fitted a responder of the principal class at
a fixed budget against each; \vsevenPrimaryFloor{}~pp is the planted cost of the smallest rung
whose recovered excess excluded zero on both banks, and the next rung down, at
\vsevenFloorRungBelow{}~pp, was not recovered. The floor is therefore bracketed by those two rungs
rather than measured as a boundary, and it is specific to that responder class, budget, handicap
family and target.

Two findings from the calibration shape the adversarial statements in this paper. First, within the
tested class and budgets, the floor did not decrease with evaluation games: an early prediction that
it would fall as (evaluation games)$^{-1/2}$ was not borne out at its own predicted value, since at
four times the games the same rung, \vsevenPrimaryFloor{}~pp, was the smallest recovered and the
next rung down remained unrecovered, with a phase-2 excess whose interval was centred near zero
rather than merely wide. This is one comparison, of two evaluation sizes for one responder class on
nested banks. For the tested responder class at these
budgets, then, more evaluation games and more fitting computation address different sources of
uncertainty. The exact result is that the \vsevenFloorRungBelow{}~pp rung was not recovered whereas
the \vsevenPrimaryFloor{}~pp rung was; no continuous detection threshold between them is estimated.
Failure to recover a small planted effect does not establish its absence, and recovery of one
handicap family does not establish sensitivity to every exploit of the same size. Second, floors are
family-specific: the declaration channel has its own and higher floor of \vsevenDeclFloor{}~pp,
measured as a win-rate response to a declaration handicap, and reading a declaration-objective
result against the general floor would overstate it. That figure is in win-rate units and is not
applied to declaration accuracy without a justified mapping of units.

An adversary class contributes to an adversarial-response statement only after passing a
planted-edge calibration at or below the effect size in question. That precondition is why the
negative controls of \S\ref{sec:results-controls} are run: an instrument that cannot recover a
planted edge of a given size cannot report the absence of a real one of that size.

\subsection{Information-access and isolation tests}
\label{sec:evaluation-side}

The evaluated team is three identical agents with no shared secret. That their policy specification
is common knowledge is legitimate (\S\ref{sec:game-why}), but it also means an implementation detail
could become an unintended signalling channel. Before this cycle the constraint had only ever been
checked by reading the source.

Four mechanical tests now run over all four engine decision types. \textbf{S3, listening
substitution} asks whether a seat's behaviour changes more when a teammate's hidden action changes
than when an opponent's does, beyond what the rules explain. \textbf{S4, stream independence} asks
whether the seat's private random stream is drawn independently of the deal. \textbf{S5, posterior
invariance} asks whether the seat's action depends on true card locations beyond what its own
posterior supports. \textbf{S6, seat isolation} replays every decision from own hand, public event
stream, rules and reset seed alone, and counts the decisions the reconstruction cannot reproduce, at
zero tolerance.

The tests are calibrated by three positive controls, each a policy carrying one planted violation,
and each must fail exactly the tests the protocol names for it in advance and no others.
\S\ref{sec:design-material} reports the calibration on holdout material. Without those controls a
clean report from the gate would be uninformative.

One limitation is stated wherever these tests are used. The zero-tolerance form of S6 requires a
single worker thread, because the audit depends on the deal-to-thread partition and work stealing
does not fix that partition at higher counts, and it requires agents rebuilt for each deal.
Rebuilding agents per deal \emph{changes play}. The fresh-agent, single-thread configuration is
therefore the one that passed the specified isolation tests. The scored configuration, with reused
agents and \vsevenScoredThreads{} worker threads, retained a cross-deal state residual in searching
agents,
so these test results do not establish strict seat isolation or exact reproducibility for the scored
mode, and every strength number in this paper is measured under that mode
(\S\ref{sec:results-controls}).

\subsection{Worst-case performance and empirical regret}
\label{sec:evaluation-panel}

The project's standing reporting rule is that the worst case leads and an aggregate never does, since
a mean over an opponent panel rewards beating weak opponents by larger margins, which is not the
quantity of interest. Two statistics are reported over a panel scored identically for every
configuration. Write $e(a,o)$ for the pooled edge of configuration $a$ against panel member $o$,
over the four evaluated configurations $a$ and the fixed panel $\mathcal{O}$. The \textbf{worst
cell} of $a$ is $\min_{o \in \mathcal{O}} e(a,o)$, and its \textbf{empirical minimax regret} is
\[
  R(a) \;=\; \max_{o \in \mathcal{O}} \Bigl[\, \max_{b} e(b,o) \;-\; e(a,o) \Bigr],
\]
the largest shortfall from the best of the four configurations on any member. Both are defined over
the evaluated configurations and the fixed panel; neither is a global minimax guarantee. They can
disagree, and in \S\ref{sec:results-panel} they do. We report both statistics because they assess
different aspects of panel performance, and report the panel mean last and labelled as a
diagnostic. Extrema and rankings computed from point estimates are descriptive: the interval printed
beside a selected worst cell is that cell's own interval, not a confidence interval for the minimum
over the panel, and no simultaneous or joint-bootstrap inference over the panel is supplied.

Every configuration must carry a cell for every member or the regret is undefined, which is why the
panel is fixed by name in advance and scored for all configurations on the same banks. The
\vsevenPanelMembers{} named members are \vsevenPanelDistinct{} distinct policies, since three
sealed aliases duplicate members that also appear under their own names. Three of the evaluated
configurations are themselves panel members and meet themselves; a self-cell is a mirror match with
an edge of exactly zero and no sampling variance. Under the higher-is-better convention a zero cell
can only lower a configuration's worst cell, which it does exactly when every other cell is
positive, and it can only raise a configuration's regret, so \S\ref{sec:results-panel} prints the
worst case both with and without self-cells so that the two can be compared; the reported regrets
are unaffected because none is attained at a self-cell.
%% --- end sections_v07/07-evaluation ---
%% --- begin sections_v07/08-threat-model ---
\section{Adversary model}
\label{sec:threat}

Robustness claims are only meaningful against a stated adversary, and the adversary appropriate to
this domain is unusual enough to define explicitly. The definition below was written before any v0.7
measurement was taken, and it constrains how \S\ref{sec:results-adversarial} may be read.

\paragraph{Evaluation unit.} What is evaluated is the joint policy $\pi = (\pi_1,\pi_2,\pi_3)$ of
three identical agents. The utility is the team win rate of \S\ref{sec:evaluation-measure}, and
parity is the benchmark: the rotation of \S\ref{sec:evaluation-duplicate} gives both teams every
deal from both sides, so under the symmetric seating of the evaluated variant a team of three copies
of the target has a 50\% win rate against itself, and a response's edge is measured relative to that.
The principal adversary controls all three opposing seats. A one-seat deviation column is reported
alongside it on the same deals, because a three-seat upgrade and a one-seat upgrade are different
quantities that this corpus has previously conflated (\S\ref{sec:results-partners}).

\paragraph{Adversary information access.} The adversary is white-box, with an explicit exclusion
list. It is given the target's source, its parameter vector, and unlimited offline access to it. Each
adversary seat observes its own hand and the public record, as the rules require; it is not given
the deal, the deal seed, any other seat's hand, or the engine's confidence field. The exclusions are
the point of the construction. Because the target team is three identical published deterministic
agents, its policy specification is common knowledge and it carries no private correlation signal,
which differs from the team-game models in which a sampled correlation signal is private to the team
\cite{celli-gatti,farina-collusion} (\S\ref{sec:game-why}). The adversary class built to exploit
that property is the transcript inverter of \S\ref{sec:results-whitebox}.

\paragraph{Team composition and channel restrictions.} The evaluated team is \emph{homogeneous} in
the sense that its three seats run identical policy specifications. Separately, two channel
prohibitions apply: the team receives no shared secret pre-play seed, and no seat may use an illegal
side channel, defined mechanically by the four tests of \S\ref{sec:evaluation-side} over all four
engine decision types. Everything else a seat-conditioned policy can do is permitted.

\paragraph{Adversary correlation regimes.} The three adversary seats may be fitted independently,
may be three copies of one vector, or may share a pre-play signal secret from the target team. The
first is the \emph{independent} regime, the second the \emph{synchronised} regime, and the third the
\emph{ex-ante correlated} regime; the last is the only one whose result would bear on the target
rather than on the search alone, and even there it remains a response within a class. It is the
registered principal case. The synchronised regime is retained as the continuity column, because it
is what earlier cycles of this corpus measured. \S\ref{sec:discussion-future} records which of the
three this study actually reached.

\paragraph{Scope of response-value estimates.} Every adversarial figure in this paper is the
observed \emph{adversarial response win-rate edge} of a particular fitted response against the
target, reported with the adversary class, the number of controlled seats, the correlation regime
and the search budget attached. A response's expected edge is no greater than the best-response
value over any class containing it, so such a figure is informative about best-response value only
as a lower bound, and only up to its sampling error: an observed point estimate is not itself a
certified bound. The tested responses do not provide an upper bound on best-response value, and
computing the true value is intractable even white-box. An estimated negative response edge is not
evidence that a conventional non-negative exploitability quantity is negative. The consequence is
stated once and observed throughout: a search that fails to find an exploit is evidence about the
search, not a property of the target. The v0.4 record in this corpus contains exactly that reading
error, having reported a null result as a demonstration of robustness, and \S\ref{app:corrections}
carries the correction rather than repeating it.

\paragraph{Adversary calibration.} An adversary class is admitted to an adversarial-response
statement only once it has passed a planted-edge calibration at or below the effect size being
claimed (\S\ref{sec:evaluation-floor}).

\paragraph{Interpretation limits.} The definition does not license the claim that a policy passing
every test is unexploitable, and it does not license reading the opponent panel's worst cell as an
exploitability figure. The panel measures robustness across playstyles; the adversary search measures
what a fitted responder achieves. They are different objects and this paper keeps them in different
subsections.
%% --- end sections_v07/08-threat-model ---
%% --- begin sections_v07/09-development ---
\section{Exploratory development}
\label{sec:development}

This section reports the development phases: what the instrument was shown to be capable of
detecting, what an open adversary search found, and which candidate mechanisms were tested. All
measurements here are on \emph{training} material and are exploratory in the sense of
\S\ref{sec:evaluation-multiplicity}. None supports the paper's primary claim, and every pooled
interval reported in this section has a lower bound below the registered
\vsevenPrimaryFloor{}~pp reference bar, so each is a measured effect rather than a confirmed one.

\subsection{Baseline selection}
\label{sec:development-frontier}

\vsix{} deployed its policy with test-time search disabled, while also having measured that search
working. The configurations available at the end of that cycle therefore form what this paper
calls the \emph{\vsix{} frontier}: the set comprising the deployed non-searching policy and the
searching configurations \fcheap{} and \fmid{}, ordered by search cost. The term is the protocol's; it
denotes that comparison set of evaluated configurations, not a measured Pareto frontier in cost
and performance, and no dominance criterion was applied.

This distinction drove the most consequential methodological choice in the programme. Several
available configurations already exceeded the deployed policy, including unfitted single-switch
deviations of it, which motivated a searching configuration as the primary baseline. The
comparison target was fixed as \fcheap{}: the \vsix{} policy with its endgame-truncated search
enabled at the same search settings \sestina{} uses, and the least expensive searching member of
the frontier. \S\ref{sec:design-protocol} records that the choice was registered in advance.
Table~\ref{tab:configs} defines every configuration this paper compares.

\subsection{Detection calibration}
\label{sec:development-instrument}

The calibration procedure of \S\ref{sec:evaluation-floor} was built in this phase, which measured
the principal responder class's floor at \vsevenFloorPhaseOne{}~pp and predicted that it would fall
as (evaluation games)$^{-1/2}$. The phase of \S\ref{sec:development-adversaries} re-measured the
same handicap ladder at four times the games: the smallest recovered rung was then the
\vsevenPrimaryFloor{}~pp rung, and the \vsevenFloorRungBelow{}~pp rung remained unrecovered, so the
prediction was not borne out within the ladder's resolution. That is the first of the two findings
\S\ref{sec:evaluation-floor} reports, the second being that floors are family-specific, with the
declaration channel's at \vsevenDeclFloor{}~pp. Two further results shape later sections.

\paragraph{Transcript-inversion diagnostics.} Because the target
team is deterministic and published, an adversary can invert its transcript, asking for each observed
ask which deals would have produced it and contracting the posterior accordingly. Measured, this
contracts the deal posterior by approximately \vsevenBitsPerAsk{} bits per observed ask beyond the
certificates the rules already supply. Converting those bits into play is worth
\vsevenInversionUnfitted{}~pp unfitted against the deployed policy.

The gain was not target-specific. The excess tracks planted-edge size to within
$\pm$\vsevenInversionTrack{}~pp on all eight rungs of the calibration ladder, which is consistent
with a general strength gain rather than with a response that depends on a particular weakness of
the target; a broadly stronger policy can still be a valid profitable response, so this is a
statement about specificity rather than about exploitability. And no linear handicap adds more
than \vsevenHandicapBits{} bits per ask, so within this policy family no readability handicap was
found for a stochastic policy to remove. A proposed stochastic policy conditional on the inverter
succeeding was therefore not pursued.

\paragraph{Revised search-cost estimates.} The \vsix{} paper priced the searching configuration
at ``three orders of magnitude'' the blueprint, a ratio that mixed an all-threads timing with a
single-thread one. Re-timed here on one basis, it is \vsevenPhaseOneCost$\times$. But that figure
describes the \emph{unrestricted} search, which is not the frontier point anything was measured at,
and phase~3 re-measured the endgame-restricted operating point at approximately
\vsevenCostRecorded$\times$. \S\ref{sec:results-cost} corrects that correction in turn.

\subsection{Adversary generation against baseline configurations}
\label{sec:development-adversaries}

The constraint on this phase was that the searches must not share a bias, since repeated restarts
of one method do not provide methodological diversity. Every exploiter search in the corpus before it
had been the same search: a cross-entropy fit of a linear vector maximising win rate from one seed
against one target. This phase constructed six adversary classes in the synchronised regime, plus
one fitted search in the ex-ante-correlated regime, and, before fitting anything against them,
measured the effect of a diagnostic intervention on each channel under the evaluated condition: a
\vsix{} target, the phase-2 responder class and budget, and the training banks.

\paragraph{Adversarial-search outcome.}
Nothing constructed produced a resolved adversarial edge against the \vsix{} frontier beyond the
class floor. The largest adversarial response edge found against the deployed policy is an in-class
figure of \vsevenBestExploit{}~pp $[\vsevenBestExploitLo, \vsevenBestExploitHi]$ over
\vsevenBestExploitN{} games, which is below the class floor of \vsevenPrimaryFloor{}. Seven
separately fitted searches gave the same picture and none cleared its class floor.

Every configuration that beat the deployed policy by more than the floor proved on measurement not
to be target-specific: the target's own test-time search at \vsevenSearchAsExploit{}~pp, and a
single out-of-class coordinate at \vsevenOneSwitchArm{}~pp whose gain survives against opponents
sharing none of the target's machinery. The second of those is the contestation weight of
\S\ref{sec:agent-contest}; \S\ref{sec:results-attribution} measures on holdout what it contributes
and finds it the largest observed leave-one-out drop among the five components \sestina{} carries,
rather than the whole of the gain.

Two further results from this phase bear on \sestina{}'s design. The declaration-urgency channel
that the phase expected to be its headline has a diagnostic intervention estimate of
\vsevenUrgencyCeiling{}~pp $[\vsevenUrgencyCeilingLo, \vsevenUrgencyCeilingHi]$ under that
condition and did not yield a resolved adversarial advantage at the tested budget, but the same channel is worth \vsevenUrgencyWorthLo{} to
\vsevenUrgencyWorthHi{}~pp to a configuration that simply disables it: the adversary owns the
smaller half of a two-point defect in the target. And the event-count cliff of
\S\ref{sec:agent-urgency} was not reached by any adversary in the tested searches, while a
configuration that removes the counter inherits a self-play game-length tail of \vsevenCliffTail{}
events, which is the obligation \S\ref{sec:agent-stall} discharges.

\subsection{Candidate-mechanism evaluation}
\label{sec:development-rejected}

Candidate modifications were evaluated on development data before configuration freezing. Five
were developed in isolated branches, gated before being scored, and checked against the
side-channel definition by the harness of \S\ref{sec:evaluation-side} rather than by inspection.
One was retained, and it is the configuration change described in \S\ref{sec:agent} rather than an
architecture. Four did not demonstrate an additional performance benefit under the tested settings:
alternative declaration allocation, learned leaf evaluation, per-decision training objectives, and
learned action ranking. The following subsections report their measured outcomes and limitations.
These results concern the tested implementations and budgets, not all methods in the corresponding
classes.

\subsubsection{Declaration-allocation comparison}
\label{sec:development-alloc}

The design register's second-ranked hypothesis, at priority \vsevenLonePriority{}, held that
\vsix{}'s entire margin lives in the declaration channel and that roughly two points of it remained.
Its proposed mechanism was that the deployed allocator, a product of per-card marginals, selects a
different allocation from the joint posterior's argmax often enough to matter.

The measurement did not support that mechanism. Over \vsevenLoneDeclsTotal{} recorded voluntary
declarations across two banks and both urgency settings, \vsevenLoneDeclsDistinct{} of them from
distinct (bank, urgency-setting) capture cells and the remainder from re-runs of the same seeds and
from the two cells of the sequential-joint policy, the sequential approximate joint's argmax and
the marginal-product argmax selected the same allocation at every one, with the joint score holding a
strict opinion on \vsevenLoneStrictLo\% to \vsevenLoneStrictHi\% of ambiguous cases.
This agreement is empirical: the two procedures are not equivalent in general, since the six cards
of a half-suit are coupled through the capacity constraint, and no argument that the sequential
conditioning preserves the marginal-product ranking is supplied.

Two further measurements bear on the direction rather than only the mechanism. First, the exact
constraint-joint maximiser \eqref{eq:bestalloc} performed worse than the deployed approximation on
the declaration decision itself: replayed on one capture cell of \vsevenJointCellDecls{} voluntary
declarations, it corrects \vsevenJointFixed{} declarations and breaks \vsevenJointBroken{}, a net
\vsevenJointWorseNet{}~pp of declaration accuracy on that cell. At the design register's fitted
conversion of about \vsevenDeclToWinConv{} win-rate points per point of declaration accuracy
(range \vsevenDeclToWinConvLo{} to \vsevenDeclToWinConvHi{} across banks), this corresponds to
approximately \vsevenJointWorseWin{}~pp of win rate; that figure is inferred from the
declaration-level difference, not measured as a game outcome. This is the second comparison of the
kind \S\ref{sec:background-vsix} reports. Second, \vsevenJointFlatLo\% to \vsevenJointFlatHi\% of
the error class occupies states in which the exact constraint-conditioned distribution assigns the
competing allocations equal probability, so most of the apparent headroom is an information limit
under that model rather than a policy defect.

The channel is also largely downstream of a defect that \sestina{} removes for other reasons.
Disabling the urgency escalation eliminates \vsevenLoneUrgencyShare\% of the entire
allocation-error class and drops the oracle-assisted estimate for a perfect allocator, at the
measured incidence, from \vsevenLoneCeilingBefore{} to \vsevenLoneCeilingAfter{}~pp, which is below
the reference bar. The
consequence is a composition constraint this paper observes: a declaration-channel gain and the
urgency-off gain should not be added, because they overlap in the same error class.

\subsubsection{Learned leaf evaluation}
\label{sec:development-leaf}

An inherited hypothesis held that the test-time search cannot be judged until its leaf evaluator is
rebuilt, the deployed one being algebraically close to a rescaling of the hit probability. A leaf was
fitted for the purpose, and the statistic that matters, between-candidate $R^2$, which is the only
component the paired deviation rule can consume, improved from \vsevenKoneRsqBefore{} to
\vsevenKoneRsqAfter{} in sample and from \vsevenKoneRsqOosBefore{} to \vsevenKoneRsqOosAfter{} out
of sample; the in-sample ratio is \vsevenKoneFold-fold, the out-of-sample ratio is much smaller,
and the absolute $R^2$ remains modest in both, so the direction replicates and the multiple does
not. The
fit was scored over approximately \vsevenKoneLeaves{} leaves across two search regimes and three
banks.

In play it is worth \vsevenKoneInPlay{}~pp against the material control's \vsevenKoneControl{}~pp,
losing on both banks individually. The diagnosis is conversion failure rather than fit failure: the
rule's deviation rate is \vsevenKoneChangeLo{} for the deployed material leaf,
\vsevenKoneChangeMid{} for the fitted one and \vsevenKoneChangeHi{} for a third arm that merely
rescales the material leaf's constant, so at the deployed shrinkage the deviation rule is nearly
leaf-invariant. The tested leaf replacement did not improve playing strength under the deployed
selection rule. This illustrates a mismatch between a surrogate metric and playing performance
related to the abstraction pathology the poker literature documents \cite{waugh-abstraction}, in
which a model improved on its own metric can leave play unchanged or worse; no formal mapping
between the two settings is claimed.

The inherited hypothesis was discharged separately and by re-measurement rather than by the new
evaluator. The single underpowered cell on which it rested, one \vsevenFullGameOldN{}-game cell at
\vsevenFullGameOld{}~pp, is the \texttt{depth=24} configuration; re-run on the training banks with
\vsix{}'s own leaf it gives \vsevenDepthTwentyFour{}
$[\vsevenDepthTwentyFourLo, \vsevenDepthTwentyFourHi]$, and full-game search at \texttt{depth=12}
gives \vsevenFullGameReOne{} and \vsevenFullGameReTwo{}~pp, pooled \vsevenFullGameRePooled{}.
Phase 1's \vsevenTruncatedEndgame{}~pp refuted the conditional in the endgame regime only, at
\texttt{depth=12} with \texttt{maxq=26}; it was this full-game cell that left it binding for
full-game search.

\subsubsection{Per-decision training objectives}
\label{sec:development-perdecision}

A further hypothesis held that a per-decision objective is necessary because the scoreboard is too
noisy to resolve small effects. Its precision claim is supported and its fitting form is not, and
the two halves are independent.

The design effect of deal clustering was measured for the first time in this cycle. A
one-point-equivalent effect reaches a planning half-width of its own size in
\vsevenPerDecisionGamesLo{} to \vsevenPerDecisionGamesHi{} games on the declaration channel and
\vsevenAllocGamesLo{} to \vsevenAllocGamesHi{} on the allocation-error channel, against
\vsevenScoreboardGames{} on the scoreboard, a ratio of \vsevenDesignEffectLo{} to
\vsevenDesignEffectHi{} times. That ratio compares planning sample sizes for different outcome
channels, linked by the design register's assumed conversion factors between per-decision accuracy
and win rate; the per-decision figures apply the measured clustering design effect and the
scoreboard figure is the independent-game approximation of \S\ref{sec:evaluation-power}, so the
ratio is a planning comparison rather than a measured precision gain on one estimand.

But all three self-oriented per-decision objectives, fitted at matched budget against a per-game
control, produced worse policies: \vsevenKfourSelfDecl{}, \vsevenKfourSelfAsk{} and
\vsevenKfourSelfAlloc{}~pp, each replicated in sign and two of them with intervals beyond the
reference bar. Each fit moved its own proxy in the intended direction and lost anyway. The
declaration-oriented fit increased declaration accuracy by \vsevenKfourTradeGain{}~pp while
reducing ask accuracy by \vsevenKfourTradeCost{}~pp, and the ask-oriented fit did the reverse. The
two per-decision channels exhibited a trade-off in these fits, so a fitter holding
\vsevenCoordinates{} coordinates improved one at the expense of the other. The results motivate
further study of the estimator without establishing the tested proxy as a suitable training
objective.

\subsubsection{Learned action ranking and tie-breaking}
\label{sec:development-tie}

In the \vsix{} mirror \vsevenTieContested\% of contested ask decisions tie bit-for-bit, and the
open question was whether anything can be \emph{selected} within that set rather than merely
randomised. The learned candidate this cycle built was not built for that question. It was the
brief's wildcard, aimed at amortising test-time search, and it was fitted unrestricted over
\vsevenKfiveFitDecisions{} searched decisions. Deployed unrestricted it scores
\vsevenKfiveUnrestricted{}~pp pooled over \vsevenPhaseThreeCell{} games, negative on both banks
$[\vsevenKfiveUnrestrictedLo, \vsevenKfiveUnrestrictedHi]$. Restricting it to the tied set is one
added switch on the same fitted weight vector, and it is there that the tie-group question gets
answered.

Restricted, against the deployed policy it scores \vsevenKfiveTieOnly{}~pp, not statistically
resolved from the free public-history tie-break's \vsevenKfiveFreeTie{}~pp; head to head against
that free tie-break at \vsevenPhaseThreeCell{} games it is \vsevenKfiveHeadToHead{}~pp $\pm$
\vsevenKfivePm{} (95\% half-width), replicated in sign. The restricted learned ranker did not
demonstrate an advantage over public-history tie-breaking, and the appropriate control for any
mechanism operating inside the tied set is the decorrelated policy rather than the deployed one.

The same instrumentation explains why. Setting every candidate's true advantage to exactly zero and
feeding the capture's real standard errors through the engine's real deviation rule reproduces the
search's observed deviation rate to a \vsevenCurseRelErr\% relative error, on a quantity the null was
never fitted to. The search's per-decision selection signal is approximately \vsevenCurseShare\%
winner's curse on its own Monte Carlo draw, and approximately \vsevenCurseTieShare\% within the tied
set, where the rule applies no shrinkage at all.

\subsubsection{Forced-endgame improvement estimates}
\label{sec:development-arithmetic}

Closing the entire forced-endgame accuracy gap at the measured incidence, re-derived from this
cycle's own capture, is estimated at \vsevenLthirteenClose{}~pp, which is the register's own figure
corrected upward by a factor of two and still a fiftieth of the reference bar. That estimate holds
for the incidence observed in the measured play. The route by which the estimate could grow is an
adversary driving the target's incidence upward, and the one construction measured does not do
that: a team that never declares voluntarily raises its \emph{own} forced-endgame rate, not its
opponent's, and pays \vsevenLthirteenAdvCostLo{} to \vsevenLthirteenAdvCostHi{}~pp for it. No
evaluated intervention increased the target's forced-endgame incidence, so this route remains
untested rather than closed (Appendix~\ref{app:corrections-self} records the earlier report's
unsupported figure for it).

\subsection{Configuration selection and freezing}
\label{sec:development-freeze}

\vsevenSelK{} configurations were scored against the deployed policy during development. One was
selected, its specification written to an artifact whose reconstruction is executed rather than
asserted, and the configuration frozen. Nothing about it was changed afterwards, and no gradient or population fit was applied to it. \S\ref{sec:results-selection} quantifies what that selection could be worth
under the null.
%% --- end sections_v07/09-development ---
%% --- begin sections_v07/10-design ---

\label{sec:design}

what was developed on training material, what was frozen, and what was measured against sealed
material.

\vsevenRtwoaGames{} games. The engine's soundness audit reports \vsevenVerifyViolations{} violations
in \vsevenVerifyChecks{} checks and its self-test passes on \vsevenSelftestChecks{} checks.

\subsection{Behavioural validation results}
\label{sec:design-gate}

\sestina{} passes all \vsevenGateRulesWord{} rules of Table~\ref{tab:gate}: \vsevenGateDeadAsks\%

\vsevenGateAskHit\% ask hit rate and \vsevenGateMisdecl\% misdeclaration. The negative control fails
\vsevenNegFailCount{} rules, namely \vsevenNegFails{}, which are exactly the rules the protocol names
for it in advance.
